power_id text
1;2 We recruited English-speaking adults located in the United States via Prolific. We posted the experimental (SMR) and control conditions as two mutually exclusive Prolific HITs with the same name and description; once a participant accepted either HIT, they were redirected to the corresponding Qualtrics survey and became ineligible for the other. As preregistered, we continued data collection until 255 participants had completed each condition. We chose this sample size to achieve 80% power to detect a small effect on the second decision, after accounting for attention-check exclusions and the reduced efficiency of the nonparametric ANCOVA used as the primary analysis. The retained sample of 435 participants, together with the observed correlation of r = .49 between the first and second decisions, provides 80% power to detect an effect of d = 0.21 in a one-tailed test at α = .05. Each group received a bonus that increased its median wage to $9.50 per hour. We excluded participants who failed any preregistered attention checks before analysis; retention was comparable across conditions.
3 The procedure for recruiting participants and assigning them to the experimental condition versus the control condition was the same as in the previous experiments. As preregistered, we aimed to continue data collection until 600 participants in each condition had passed the attention checks; after final data cleaning, slightly fewer analyzable participants remained (596 and 599; see Participants). Because the effect of interest in this experiment is transfer to a second dilemma involving a different outgroup, we set this target to be sensitive to an effect smaller than the within-domain effects of Experiments 1 and S1. The attained sample size provides 80% power to detect an effect of d = 0.14 in a one-tailed test at α = .05.
4 Our rationale for the target sample size was as follows. Anticipating that effects diminish over time, we aimed for 80% power to detect effects of d = 0.14 or larger at α = .05 in one-sided tests, adjusting for each outcome’s pre-intervention value. Using pilot data, we estimated that the correlation between pre-and post-intervention scores would be at least 0.61. With these inputs, ChatGPT (OpenAI, 2025-2026) calculated that we would need about 400 analyzable participants per condition. We recruited 600 per condition to allow for data-quality exclusions and for attrition at the one-week follow-up.
5 For the pair of social dilemmas used in Experiment S2, SMR had no detectable effect on this second decision. Participants who reflected donated about the same amount (M = $70,720, SD = $51,200) as participants who did not (M = $71,150, SD = $45,570), t(940.73) = -0.14, p = .889, d = -0.01, 95% CI [-0.14, 0.12] (Figure 2D). The preregistered nonparametric ANCOVA also found no difference between the two conditions’ regression curves (h = 0.09, p = .992), and an exploratory test found no evidence that the curves were not parallel (h = 0.09, p = .966). This null result does not reflect insufficient power. With 966 participants the design had 80% power to detect an effect of d = 0.18 in a two-tailed test, and the confidence interval excludes the effects observed in Experiment 1 (d = 0.43, 95% CI [0.24, 0.62]), Experiment S1 (d = 0.31, 95% CI [0.12, 0.50]) and Experiment 2 (d = 0.37, 95% CI [0.26, 0.48]).

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 10 imprecise p values out of 228 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (e.g., “decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

P-Value Text
p > .999 Reflection raised volunteering uniformly, with no evidence against parallel regression curves (h = 1.61, p > .999).
p ≥ .55 Notably, SMR had no effect on moral universalism (Enke et al., 2022), that is, on how participants allocated money between more and less socially distant strangers in the absence of any information about their needs (all p ≥ .55).
ps ≥ .100 Participants’ self-reported reliance on deliberation, moral rules, and costbenefit reasoning was unaffected (all ps ≥ .100; see Table S2.11.1).
ps ≥ .114 The remaining specific indirect effects were not significant (all ps ≥ .114).
ps ≥ .65 The difference between SMR and the comparison conditions also faded away for donations to charities seeking to restrict the rights of transgender people (all one-tailed ps ≥ .65, dadj = 0.06, 95% CI [-0.06, 0.18] for EI and dadj = 0.02, 95% CI [-0.10, 0.14] for PT).
ps ≥ .18 At follow-up, the SMR condition no longer significantly differed from either comparison condition with respect to policy support (one-tailed ps ≥ .18; dadj=0.02, 95% CI [-0.06, 0.11] for EI; dadj=0.00, 95% CI [-0.09, 0.09] for PT), moral-circle placement (one-tailed ps ≥ .34; dadj=0.00, 95% CI [-0.10, 0.09] for EI; dadj=0.02, 95% CI [-0.08, 0.12] for PT), or impartial beneficence (one-tailed ps ≥ 0.84, dadj = -0.07, 95% CI [-0.20, 0.05] for EI, and dadj = -0.07, 95% CI [-0.19, 0.06] for PT).
ps ≥ .34 At follow-up, the SMR condition no longer significantly differed from either comparison condition with respect to policy support (one-tailed ps ≥ .18; dadj=0.02, 95% CI [-0.06, 0.11] for EI; dadj=0.00, 95% CI [-0.09, 0.09] for PT), moral-circle placement (one-tailed ps ≥ .34; dadj=0.00, 95% CI [-0.10, 0.09] for EI; dadj=0.02, 95% CI [-0.08, 0.12] for PT), or impartial beneficence (one-tailed ps ≥ 0.84, dadj = -0.07, 95% CI [-0.20, 0.05] for EI, and dadj = -0.07, 95% CI [-0.19, 0.06] for PT).
ps ≥ 0.84 At follow-up, the SMR condition no longer significantly differed from either comparison condition with respect to policy support (one-tailed ps ≥ .18; dadj=0.02, 95% CI [-0.06, 0.11] for EI; dadj=0.00, 95% CI [-0.09, 0.09] for PT), moral-circle placement (one-tailed ps ≥ .34; dadj=0.00, 95% CI [-0.10, 0.09] for EI; dadj=0.02, 95% CI [-0.08, 0.12] for PT), or impartial beneficence (one-tailed ps ≥ 0.84, dadj = -0.07, 95% CI [-0.20, 0.05] for EI, and dadj = -0.07, 95% CI [-0.19, 0.06] for PT).
ps ≥ .57 Moreover, SMR participants did not give the pro-transgender charity a larger share when splitting a donation between it and an arts, disaster-relief, or local-community charity (bs from -$1.45 to -$0.31, all one-tailed ps ≥ .57).
ps < .01 In direct, two-tailed comparisons with perspective taking, SMR produced significantly greater transfer effects on willingness to interact with immigrants, dehumanization of immigrants, warmth toward immigrants, and the weight placed on the welfare of LGB people (dadj = 0.10 to 0.15, all ps < .01).

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

To check (2)

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 95 non-significant p values that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

Aczel B, Palfi B, Szollosi A, Kovacs M, Szaszi B, Szecsi P, Zrubka M, Gronau Q, van den Bergh D, Wagenmakers E (2018). “Quantifying Support for the Null Hypothesis in Psychology: An Empirical Investigation.” Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science, 1(3), 357–366. doi:10.1177/2515245918773742.

Murphy S, Merz R, Reimann L, Fernández A (2025). “Nonsignificance misinterpreted as an effect’s absence in psychology: Prevalence and temporal analyses.” Royal Society Open Science, 12(3), 242167. doi:10.1098/rsos.242167.

For educational material on preventing the misinterpretation of p values, see Improving Your Statistical Inferences.

More detail

Ethics Approval Check

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, and it was present:

Finally, all participants were English-speaking adults in the United States responding in brief online studies, and the groups they considered were largely vulnerable people in need; whether the intervention works in other countries and languages, for other kinds of outgroups, for other types of consequential real-experiments are available in the project’s OSF repository at https://osf.io/kgx7e/overview?

After seeing the outcomes of their first decision, half of the participants were guided to systematically reflect on it, and then all of them made a second, comparable decision.

We recruited English-speaking adults located in the United States via Prolific.

We posted the experimental (SMR) and control conditions as two mutually exclusive Prolific HITs with the same name and description; once a participant accepted either HIT, they were redirected to the corresponding Qualtrics survey and became ineligible for the other.

As preregistered, we continued data collection until 255 participants had completed each condition.

After the first decision, all participants were shown the outcome of their decision for both groups.

Participants were first asked to list all potential consequences they had considered.

Immediately afterward, all participants were shown the outcome of that decision for all affected parties, including both the outgroup and the protagonist’s ingroup.

Finally, all participants completed four true/false attention-check questions assessing their comprehension of the scenario, and provided feedback on the study.

We recruited 1,547 participants on Prolific Academic.

All participants were Englishspeaking adults located in the USA.

These participants were evenly distributed across both conditions (596 in the control condition and 599 in the experimental condition).

In this decision, participants had to trade off benefits to foodinsecure beneficiaries against costs to themselves and their family and friends.

Then, all participants were informed of the outcome of their decision and answered two attention-check questions.

In this decision, participants had to trade off the potential benefit to sick, underprivileged children against the benefit to themselves and their families.

In the shoes of an accountant who was awarded a $4,995 holiday bonus check for excellent work completed that year, participants had the choice to donate to the local children’s hospital to help fund new surgical equipment or spend it on booking a family vacation to Bali.

We measured how much participants had relied on moral intuition, deliberation, moral rules, and cost-benefit reasoning when making the second decision using the 12-item Decision Process Scale developed by Cheung et al. (2026) on a scale from 0 (not at all) to 100 (entirely).

Participants were presented with a list of individuals from various social groups, comprising a family member, a close friend, a coworker, a community member, an elderly community member, a special needs community member, a children’s hospital patient in the community (the outgroup in the second decision), and a complete stranger.

Participants were asked to place each individual in one of the four circles of moral concern from the moral expansiveness scale (Crimston et al., 2016)

Participants completed the short-form of the social discounting task developed by Amormino et al. (2025).

We aimed to recruit 2,400 adult participants on Prolific (600 per condition).

For ethical and scientific reasons, we used Prolific’s screening function to restrict participation to adult US citizens residing in the US who are fluent in English, do not identify as LGBTQ+, and have an approval rate of at least 95% on Prolific.

We recruited 600 per condition to allow for data-quality exclusions and for attrition at the one-week follow-up.

Because these participants did not receive the intended manipulation, we excluded their data from all analyses; we compensated them in full, and we recruited replacement participants.

Of the 2,424 randomized participants who completed the survey, 114 were excluded for failing more than one of three attention and comprehension checks, completing the survey in less than one-third of their condition’s median completion time, or not being confidently classified as unassisted human respondents by Prolific’s authenticity checks;

Based on Prolific’s demographic records, 65.1% of participants were White, 16.9% Black, 7.4% of mixed ethnicity, 6.3% Asian, and 3.1% from other ethnicities (1.2% unavailable or undisclosed); all resided in the United States, 91.9% were born there, and 45.1% were employed full-time.

We preregistered that Part 1 data collection would continue until 2400 participants had completed the first survey or until 14 days after launch, whichever came first.

As preregistered, Part 2 data collection closed 7 days after the last Part 1 participants were invited to Part 2.

After providing informed consent, participants were asked whether they identified as transgender and whether they identified as an immigrant in the USA.

Next, participants completed the postintervention measures.

Because the Prolific study initially linked to an outdated draft of the survey, 80 participants (6%) first completed a version that did not record the welfare tradeoff ratios; Section S1.7.3 of the Supplemental Materials describes how we handled this, and Section S1.7 reports the full methods of Part 3.

Participants were shown an image of a transgender woman with pink hair in a thinking posture sitting on a couch in front of a designer table with a laptop, pencils, paper, and a cellphone. 3Participants were then asked to write a brief passage describing a typical day in the life of the individual in the photograph from a first-person perspective.

Participants were prompted to write about the woman’s goals and intentions as well as her thoughts and feelings.

Of the 655 participants assigned to the perspective-taking condition, 608 (92.8%) completed the writing exercise and the subsequent self-reports; their passages averaged 148.7 words (SD = 47.2).

To enforce engagement with these prompts, participants had to spend at least 100 seconds on the page and had to enter at least 20 characters.

Of the 653 participants assigned to the empathy-induction condition, 624 (95.6%) completed the writing task, spending a median of 186 s on the intervention page (the video itself lasted 1 min 17 s); the median participant wrote 22 words.

For each of the four people, participants were asked to imagine they could increase their own well-being and the well-being of that other person.

Each participant completed the first two blocks in random order: one before the intervention and one after.

This unexpected finding appears to contradict our findings that SMR increased support for laws protecting transgender people’s rights, donations to charities helping transgender people, and the weight participants assigned to the welfare of transgender people.

A parallel-mediation analysis tested whether the effect of SMR on how much people were willing to donate to help transgender people after the intervention was mediated by the eight types of moral lessons while controlling for how much each participant had donated to help transgender people before the intervention (see Table S2.2.4 for full results).

The analyses presented in this section indicate that the SMR’s persistent effect on donations was carried by the sustained increase in the weight participants placed on transgender people’s welfare and the lessons participants had learned.

Its second decision recast the first decision’s outgroup-food-insecure Americans, whom some participants had resolved to prioritize-as more similar to the second decision’s ingroup than to its outgroup.

SMR consistently increased the attention participants paid to potential consequences for distant others and the weight they put on those consequences.

Its effect on donations one week later survived adjustment for all post-intervention measures of participants’ feelings and beliefs about the outgroup; the weight participants assigned to the outgroup’s welfare was the only pathway that carried a significant indirect effect.

First, the effects transferred to incentive-compatible donations, where participants gave up their own money for the stakeholders whose welfare they had reflected on, echoing earlier findings that moral learning transferred to an incentive-compatible donation in a separate experiment run by a different, seemingly unrelated experimenter (Maier et al., 2026).

Additionally, the question asking participants to put themselves in those people’s shoes as they experienced the consequences was split into two questions: participants were guided to first think the other person’s thoughts and then feel their feelings.

In total, 2,208 participants completed Part 2.

Of these, 55 were excluded based on the same preregistered data-quality criteria of Part 2 (failing more than 1 of the comprehension and attention checks or speeding), and a further 146 were excluded for not meeting the inclusion criteria of Part 1 (including participants affected by the technical error).

About four and a half months after Part 1, we invited all participants from the Part 1 analytic sample whose Prolific accounts were still active (2,308 of 2,310), regardless of whether they had completed Part 2.

ProcedureFor Part 1, we posted two mutually exclusive Prolific HITs with identical names in parallel (1200 participants each).

Ethics approval statement:

All experiments were approved by the UCLA Office of the Human Research Protection Program (protocols IRB-25-1758 and IRB-25-2643).

UCLA Office of the Human Research Protection Program under protocol numbers IRB-25-1758 (Moral Reflection; Experiments 1, 2, S1, and S2) and IRB-25-2643 (SMR Effect on Outgroup Attitudes; Experiment 3).

research_questions H1: After people are guided to systematically reflect on the social impact of a decision they made in a previous us-vs-them dilemma, their decision in a subsequent analogous us-vs-them dilemma is more impartially altruistic than the decision of people who did not engage in systematic moral reflection. H1: Compared with neutral reflection on leisure activities (NR), systematic moral reflection (SMR) will increase: (a) donations to a charity helping transgender people; (b) reduced donations to charities restricting transgender rights; (c) warmth toward transgender people; (d) perceived importance of transgender welfare; (e) support for policies protecting/expanding transgender rights; (f) moral-circle inclusion of transgender people; (g) willingness to choose a transgender person as a social partner (reduced stigma).
H1h: H1a-g persist controlling for self-other overlap.
H2: Compared with NR, SMR will increase the proportion of transgender-earmarked donations allocated to the more effective intervention for helping transgender people.
H3: Compared with NR, SMR will increase transfer outcomes: (a) endorsement of impartial beneficence; (b) donations to effective charities helping immigrants/refugees/foreigners; (c) perceived importance of immigrants’ welfare; (d) willingness to form social relationships with immigrants (reduced stigma); e) WTR for LGB people, f) moral concern for LGB people.
H4: Compared with NR, SMR will increase the proportion of refugee-earmarked donations allocated to the more effective intervention for helping refugees.
H5: Compared with empathy induction and perspective-taking, SMR will increase: (a) donations to charities helping transgender people; (b) reduced donations to charities restricting transgender rights; (c) support for transgender-rights policies; (d) willingness to choose a transgender person as a social partner; (e) moral-circle inclusion of transgender people; (f) perceived importance of transgender welfare; (g) warmth toward transgender people; (h) endorsement of impartial beneficence.
H6: SMR teaches people to help transgender people more effectively than empathy induction or perspective-taking.
H7: When splitting a donation between an effective charity helping people in Africa and a less effective opportunity to help one or more transgender people, SMR will allocate a greater share to the effective charity than empathy induction.
H8: SMR teaches people to help foreigners more effectively than empathy induction or perspective-taking.
H9: Moral lessons learned through SMR mediate a significant share of SMR’s effect on donations to the charity helping transgender people, beyond perceived oneness and empathic concern.
H10: SMR’s effect on donations to charities helping transgender people is partially mediated by increased welfare-tradeoff ratios for transgender people.
H11: SMR’s effect on donations to charities helping transgender people is partially mediated by increased impartial beneficence. 		This preregistration extends AsPredicted 288,741, which governs the immediate-outcome analyses. It covers the added 1-week follow-up and longitudinal analyses.
H1: Relative to neutral reflection (NR), 1 week after systematic moral reflection (SMR), participants will: (a) donate more to a charity helping transgender people; (b) donate less to charities restricting transgender rights; (c) feel warmer toward transgender people; (d) assign greater importance to transgender welfare; (e) express more support for policies protecting/expanding transgender rights; (f) place transgender people closer to the center of their moral circle; (g) be more willing to choose a transgender person as a social partner (less stigma).
H1h: H1a-g persist controlling for self-other overlap with transgender people.
H2: Relative to NR, SMR increases the effectiveness of financial support for transgender people.
H3: Relative to NR, SMR increases transfer outcomes: (a) endorsement of impartial beneficence; (b) donations to effective charities helping immigrants/refugees/foreigners; (c) perceived importance of immigrants’ welfare; (d) willingness to form social relationships with immigrants (less stigma), e) moral concern for immigrants, f) WTR for LGB people, g) moral concern for LGB people.
H4: Relative to NR, SMR increases the effectiveness of financial support for foreigners.
H5: Relative to empathy induction (EI) and perspective-taking (PT), SMR participants will: (a) donate more to charities helping transgender people; (b) donate less to charities restricting transgender rights; (c) express more support for transgender-rights policies; (d) be more willing to choose a transgender person as a social partner; (e) place transgender people closer to the center of their moral circle; (f) assign greater importance to transgender welfare; (g) feel warmer toward transgender people; (h) endorse impartial beneficence more strongly.
H6: SMR teaches people to help transgender people more effectively than EI or PT.
H7: When splitting a donation between an effective charity helping people in Africa and a less effective opportunity to help one or more transgender people, SMR allocates a greater share to the effective charity than EI or PT.
H8: SMR teaches people to help foreigners more effectively than EI or PT.
H9: Relative to EI, SMR participants will show greater persistence (less decay from immediate post-test to 1-week follow-up) on: a) donations to charities helping transgender people; (b) donations to charities restricting transgender rights; (c) warmth toward transgender people; (d) importance assigned to transgender welfare; (e) support for policies protecting/expanding transgender rights; (f) moral-circle placement of transgender people; (g) willingness to choose a transgender person as a social partner; (h) endorsement of impartial beneficence. 		Do the effects of SMR on behavior and attitudes toward the target outgroup persist more than 3 months after the intervention, and how far they generalize?

All hypotheses are about the data collected in Part 3.

Primary hypotheses (each directional, SMR vs. neutral-reflection control (NR)):
1. H1: SMR participants will exhibit higher welfare-tradeoff ratios (WTR) for transgender people than NR participants.
2. H2: SMR participants will show less blatant dehumanization of transgender people than NR participants.
3. H3 : SMR participants will allocate a larger share of a $100 donation split to a charity helping transgender people versus an arts/culture charity than NR participants.
4. H4: SMR participants experience stronger positive moral obligations towards transgender people.
5. H5: SMR participants donate more money to charities helping transgender people
6. H6 (persistence of transfer):
1. SMR participants will exhibit higher welfare-tradeoff ratios for LGB people.
2. SMR participants will exhibit less dehumanization of immigrants.
Secondary directional hypotheses (each SMR > NR):
1. S1 warmth toward transgender people
2. S2 willingness to interact with transgender people
3. S3 allocation share to the pro-transgender charity versus a local-community charity
4. S4 signing the solidarity letter (see 3)
5. S5 writing a deliverable supportive note (see 3).
Further secondary hypothesis:
6. S6: For each DV of H1-H6, the mean will be higher in the SMR condition than in the Empathy Induction (EI) or the Perspective Taking (PT) conditions.
7. S7: No effect of SMR vs. NR on the self-vs-arts donation.
8. S8 (vulnerability gradient; ordered prediction): the effect of SMR (vs. NR) on the three pure self-vs-charity donations is ordered: pro-transgender >= never-targeted vulnerable group (disaster relief) > arts/culture.
9. S9: SMR participants will allocate a larger share of the $100 split between the pro-transgender charity and the disaster-relief charity to the pro-transgender charity than NR participants. 		H1: After people are guided to systematically reflect on the social impact of a decision they made in a previous social dilemma, their decision in a subsequent analogous social dilemma is more altruistic than the decision of people who did not engage in systematic moral reflection.

H2: The effect of systematic reflection on volunteering is partially mediated by an increase in people’s reliance on utilitarian reasoning.

H3: The effect of systematic reflection on volunteering is partially mediated by a reduction in the relative importance of the decision’s effects on friends and family. 		H1: After people are guided to systematically reflect on the social impact of a decision they made in a previous social dilemma, their decision in a subsequent analogous social dilemma is more altruistic than the decision of people who did not engage in systematic moral reflection.
H2: The effect of systematic reflection on money donated is partially mediated by an increase in people’s reliance on utilitarian reasoning.
H3: The effect of systematic reflection on money donated is partially mediated by a reduction in the relative importance of the decision’s effects on people they know. 		H1: After people are guided to systematically reflect on the social impact of a decision they made in a previous us-vs-them dilemma, their decision in a subsequent analogous us-vs-them dilemma is more impartially altruistic than the decision of people who did not engage in systematic moral reflection.
design_dependent_variables SECOND_DECISION: amount of money people say they would give to charity in the second social dilemma DONATIONS: The amount of money donated to the charity helping/harming transgender people / immigrants is the amount donated in the one post-test donation where the participant allocated money between themselves and a charity helping/harming transgender people / immigrants.
Perceived moral standing: The moral circle in which the participant placed the entity they could benefit in the volunteering decision in an adapted version of the moral circle task from the MES scale (Crimston et al., 2016). We will estimate the moral concern for each entity by its distance ot the center of the moral circles.
The importance of the welfare of an outgroup will be measured by its WELFARE TRADEOFF RATIO, which is the position of the lambda slider in the welfare allocation task by Qi et al. (2025). Participants indicate how much well-being they and the other entity should receive. We measure the welfare tradeoff ratio for the entity that the participant could help in the volunteering dilemma.
The moral lessons will be extracted from participants’ written summary of the lesson they have learned using automatic thematic analysis and content analysis.
Policy support: Items for policies on restricting the rights of transgender people will be reverse-coded. Then, the average agreement score will be calculated.
Willingness to engage in social interactions / social distance/stigma: The reported likelihood ratings (1-7) will be averaged across all social interactions included in the scale. 		At the one-week follow-up, we will re-administer the following outcome measures described in preregistration 288,741: main donation/allocation tasks using parallel forms; warmth toward transgender people and immigrants; policy support for transgender rights; social distance/willingness for social interaction for transgender people and immigrants; moral-circle placement for transgender people and immigrants; WTR / importance of welfare for transgender people and immigrants; impartial beneficence; self–other overlap with transgender people; support for anti-immigration charities In order of administration (behavioral measures before attitude measures):
1. Donation decisions (incentive-compatible; as in Waves 1-3, two randomly selected participants have one decision executed). Six $100 allocations. Three pure decisions split $100 between the participant and (a) a charity helping transgender people, (b) a charity serving a vulnerable group never targeted in this study (disaster victims), or (c) an arts/culture organization. Three split decisions divide $100 between two charities: (d) pro-transgender vs. arts/culture (H3), (e) pro-transgender vs. disaster relief (S9), (f) pro-transgender vs. the participant’s local community (S3). Instances per type - pro-transgender: Advocates for Trans Equality Education Fund, FORGE, Transgender Health and Wellness Center; disaster relief: Direct Relief, Team Rubicon, All Hands and Hearts; arts/culture: Americans for the Arts, National Trust for Historic Preservation, League of American Orchestras; local community: the United Way chapter or Habitat for Humanity affiliate serving the participant’s area. Each participant sees one randomly assigned instance per type (instance indicators serve as covariates). All instances are 4/4-star rated on Charity Navigator, none appeared in Waves 1-3, and none relates to the intervention materials. Order is randomized within the pure and split blocks.
2. Welfare-tradeoff ratio (WTR) for transgender people (H1); same instrument as Waves 1-3.
3. Blatant dehumanization of transgender people (H2; Ascent-of-Man scale, as in Waves 1-3).
4. Feeling thermometer (S1) and willingness to interact (S2); same instruments as Waves 1-3.
5. Solidarity letter (S4; real behavior). Binary choice to add one’s first name (or anonymous support) to a collective letter of support for transgender people that the research team will submit to You Are Loved Here (youarelovedhere.org), which delivers letters of support to transgender subscribers by email. The letter will be sent as described.
6. Supportive note (S5; real behavior). Optional writing of a brief supportive note to a transgender person. Notes passing a content screen (supportive, non-political, no identifying information) will be printed and mailed to Point of Pride’s letter-writing program (pointofpride.org/letter-writing), which includes them in support packages for transgender people. DV: wrote a deliverable note (binary). Notes will be delivered as described.
7. Exploratory: (i) binary choice of whether a brief informational message about the situation of transgender people (selected or written by the participant) is shown to 10 future study participants (text retained for content analysis); (ii) 3 yes/no retrospective items on outgroup-relevant behavior since Wave 3 (donated, spoke up, shared information). 		VOLUNTEERING_POSTTEST: number of hours people say they would volunteer in the second social dilemma

UTILITARIAN_POSTTEST: the degree to which the participant said they tried to maximize the sum of everyone’s well-being when they made the second decision

IMPORTANCE_FRIENDS_AND_FAMILY_POSTTEST: participants’ self-report of how important considerations about potential effects on friends and family were for their second decision 		SECOND_DECISION: amount of money people say they would give to charity in the second social dilemma
UTILITARIAN_POSTTEST: the degree to which the participant said they tried to maximize the sum of everyone’s well-being when they made the second decision
DIFF_MAX_COMPELLINGNESS_OUTVSIN: the difference between the most important consequences participants considered in the second decision that affects people they don’t know and the most important consequence participants considered that affects friends and family, or other people they know 		SECOND_DECISION: amount of money people say they would give to charity in the second social dilemma
study_design_overview The experiment will have two conditions:
1. Experimental condition: Participants engage in systematic moral reflection on the outcomes of the first decision before moving on to the second decision.
2. Control condition: Participants move on to the second decision immediately after the first decision. 		The experiment will have four conditions:
Systematic Moral Reflection: Participants engage in systematic moral reflection on the outcomes of the first decision before moving on to the second decision.
Neutral Reflection: Participants reflect on leisure activities.
Empathy Induction
Perspective Taking
We will post two HITs that are mutually exclusive and will be available at the same time to the same participants. Participants accepting the first HIT will be randomly assigned to either condition 1 or condition 2. Participants accepting the second HIT will be randomly assigned to condition 3 or condition 4. The random assignment will use counter-balancing. 		The same follow-up survey will be administered to all four conditions from preregistration 288,741. No new assignment. Participants remain in the four conditions to which they were randomly assigned in Experiment 1a.8: systematic moral reflection (SMR), neutral reflection (NR), empathy induction (EI), and perspective taking (PT). All Wave 2 completers of all four conditions whose data from Waves 1-2 meets the inclusion criteria will be re-invited with a generic invitation (“a follow-up survey about social decisions”) that does not mention transgender people, immigrants, or the earlier intervention. The primary contrast for H1–H6, S1–S5, S7, S8, and S9 is SMR vs. NR; EI and PT serve the comparative-durability hypothesis (S6). The experiment will have two conditions:

statistical_tests H1 will be tested using a non-parametric ANCOVA analysis in R using the “sm” package: sm.ancova(first_decision,second_decision,group,model=“equal”).
In all analyses, the alpha level will be 0.05. 		H1-H4:
Subset data to the neutral reflection and SMR conditions.
Fit a linear model in R using stats::lm.
Extract the t-value for the SMR effect and its residual df.
Compute the one-tailed p-value from the t distribution.
Models/effects of interest: H1a-d, H2, H3b-c, H3e-f, H4: DV_post ~ group * DV_pre
H1e-f: DV ~ group * WTR_target_pre + warmth_transgender_pre
H1g: DV ~ group * warmth_transgender_pre + donation_transgender_pre + gender
H3d: DV ~ group * warmth_immigrants_pre + donation_immigrants_pre + immigrant_identity + gender
H3a: impartial_beneficence ~ group + groupWTR_transgender_pre + groupWTR_immigrant_pre
H3b-c
H1h: H1a-g models plus post-intervention self-other overlap as an additional covariate. If DV_pre measures negative attitudes (H1b, H5b, H5d, and mediation analyses of those effects), it will be centered at its pretest mean across all conditions.
H5: Use the full sample and replace the binary group variable with a condition factor with SMR as the reference level. Conduct one-tailed tests on each non-SMR coefficient. Higher/lower SMR scores correspond to negative/positive coefficients; the alternative hypothesis will be set accordingly.
H6: Apply the H5 approach to the DV in H6.
H7: Apply the H5 approach to the DV in H6, but evaluate only the empathy-induction coefficient.
H8: Apply the H5 approach to the proportion allocated to the effective charity in the split between an immigrant GoFundMe campaign and an effective global health/development charity.
H9: Estimate a parallel multiple-mediator model in lavaan. Mediators: empathy, oneness, and one variable for the count of each theme identified by automatic thematic analysis. H9 is supported if the total indirect effect via moral lessons is significantly greater than 0.
H10-H11: Use causal mediation analysis with mediation::mediate, controlling for the DV pretest value. The mediator is posttest WTR favoring the donation beneficiary (H10) or posttest impartial beneficence (H11). R code: med_model <- lm(mediator_post ~ group + WTR_transgender_pre + WTR_transgender_pre:group, data=data_frame) out_model <- lm(DV_post ~ group + mediator_post + DV_pre + WTR_transgender_pre + group:WTR_transgender_pre, data=data_frame) med_out <- mediate(med_model, out_model, treat=“group”, mediator=“mediator_post”, boot=TRUE, sims=10000) summary(med_out)
Alpha=.05 for all analyses. All directional hypotheses will be tested one-tailed. 		Immediate-outcome analyses will be conducted as specified in AsPredicted 288,741. The present preregistration concerns the follow-up-wave outcomes and the longitudinal analyses.
H1–H4 will be tested as follows: Subset the data to the neutral reflection (control) and SMR conditions. Fit a linear model in R (stats::lm). Extract the t-value for the SMR effect and its residual df. Compute the one-tailed p-value using the t CDF.
Models/effects of interest: H1a–d, H2: DV_fu ~ group * DV_pre H1e–f: DV_fu ~ group * WTR_target_pre + warmth_transgender_pre
H1g: DV_fu ~ group * warmth_transgender_pre + donation_transgender_pre + gender
H1h: H1a–g models + an additional covariate for follow-up self–other overlap with transgender people. If DV_pre indexes negative attitudes (H1b, H5b, H5d), center it at its pre-test sample mean across all conditions.
H3a: impartial_beneficence_fu ~ group + group x WTR_transgender_pre + group x WTR_immigrant_pre + group x WTR_LGB_pre
H3b-c: DV_fu ~ group x DV_pre + ImmigrantIdentity
H3d: immigrant_stigma_fu ~ group * warmth_immigrants_pre + donation_immigrants_pre + WTR_immigrant_pre + ImmigrantIdentity + gender
H3e: MES_Immigrants_fu ~ group * WTR_immigrant_pre_c + ImmigrantIdentity_ec
H3f: MES_LGB_fu ~ group * WTR_LGB_pre_c + gender_ec
H3g: WTR_LGB_fu ~ group * WTR_LGB_pre_c + gender_ec

H4: DV_fu ~ group * DV_pre + ImmigrantIdentity + gender_ec
H5: Use the full dataset (all groups) and replace the binary group with a condition factor using SMR as the baseline. Conduct one-sided t-tests on each non-SMR condition coefficient; higher/lower SMR corresponds to a negative/positive coefficient, and the alternative hypothesis is set accordingly. Aside from these changes, the statistical models will be the same as those used to analyze the same DVs for H1 and H3.
H6-H7: Apply the H5 approach to the DV in H6 & H7, evaluating only the empathy-induction coefficient.
H8: Apply the H5 approach to the proportion allocated to the effective charity in the split between an effective and an ineffective way to help immigrants/refugees/foreigners at follow-up.
H9: Fit a linear mixed-effects model to each DV: DV ~ group * time + DV_pre + [baseline covariates from the corresponding model from H1-H8] + (1 | participant), where condition is a four-level factor (NR, SMR, EI, PT) with EI coded as the reference level, and time is a two-level factor with post-test coded as the reference level. Then conduct a one-sided test on the coefficient of the condition[SMR] × time[follow-up] interaction.
Missing follow-up data will not be imputed. Alpha = 0.05 for all analyses. All directional hypotheses will be tested one-tailed. All continuous pre-intervention measures will be grand-mean centered. All binary covariates will be effect-coded as +0.5 vs. -0.5. 		All continuous covariates will be mean-centered and all categorical covariates will be effect-coded.
H1, H2, H5: mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM) using all available observations from all waves. The DV at Waves 2-4 is modeled with condition, wave (categorical), their interaction, the mean-centered Wave-1 baseline and its interactions with wave and condition (for H2, which has no Wave-1 measure, the mean-centered pre-intervention attitude covariates from the pre-registration of Part 2 are used instead), gender, and (for H5) charity-instance indicators; estimation by restricted maximum likelihood with an unstructured residual covariance across waves and Kenward-Roger degrees of freedom. Test: one-tailed test of the condition contrast at Wave 4, α = .05; we will report d_adj with its 95% CI. If the unstructured covariance does not converge, we will fit, in this order, heterogeneous Toeplitz, heterogeneous AR(1), and heterogeneous compound symmetry, and use the first structure that converges.
H6: The approach from H1 will be applied separately to the DVs from H6a and H6b. We will. consider H6 confirmed if either tests is significant after a Holm correction for two comparisons.
H3 and H4: OLS regression of each Wave-4 outcome on condition (SMR vs. NR), adjusting for pre-treatment covariates only: participant gender; the Wave-1 (baseline) measure of the same construct (for H4 that is the MES rating for transgender people); and charity-instance indicators where applicable; the model will also include the interaction effect between condition and the pre-test score. Test: one-tailed t-test of the condition coefficient, α = .05. We will report adjusted standardized effect sizes with 95% CIs.
S1-S3: the H3-H4 model form and covariates, one-tailed t-test of the condition coefficient, α = .05.
S4 and S5 (binary outcomes): logistic regression with the covariates and interaction from H1-H5, one-tailed, α = .05.
S6: for each of the H1-H4 outcomes, the same models with EI or PT in place of NR (same covariates and interaction effect), one-tailed, α = .05.
S7: the H3-H4 model form applied to the pure self-vs-arts donation. We will compute a 90% CI on the condition effect; if the 90% CI falls entirely within +/-0.20 SD, we will consider the hypothesis supported.
S8: Each ordered difference will be tested by applying the H3-H4 procedure to the corresponding within-participant difference score (pro-transgender - disaster-relief allocation; disaster-relief minus arts/culture allocation).
S9: the H3-H4 model form applied to the share of split decision (e) allocated to the pro-transgender charity; one-tailed t-test of the condition coefficient, α = .05. H2 and H3 will be tested using causal mediation analysis with the mediate function of the R package “mediation”. The first decision (first_decision) will be included as a covariate in both the mediator model and the outcome model. The number of simulations will be set to 10,000.

In all analyses, the alpha level will be 0.05. 		H1 will be tested using a non-parametric ANCOVA analysis in R using the “sm” package: sm.ancova(first_decision,second_decision,group,model=“equal”)

outliers_and_exclusions We will exclude all participants who failed one or more of the 2 attention check questions in the middle or one or more of the the 2 attention check questions at the end of the study, and all participants who did not complete those attention checks. We will exclude all participants who failed more than one of the other attention checks, all participants who did not complete those attention checks, as well as participants who completed the survey in less than ⅓ of the median completion time of all participants in the same condition.
Participants who failed the attention check about the goals of the charities will be excluded from all analyses involving donations to those charities.
Participants who fail the attention check embedded in the policy support questions will be excluded from all analyses of policy support.
Participants who report identifying as transgender will be excluded.
We will only include participants for whom Prolific’s authenticity checks confidently indicate they are not bots and didn’t use LLMs. 		We will only include participants for whom Prolific’s authenticity checks indicate are not bots with high confidence. Participants must also complete the Stage 2 survey to be eligible for inclusion in the analyses described here. We will exclude all participants who did not meet the inclusion criteria of Stage 1, failed more than one of the attention checks, did not complete those attention checks, or completed the follow-up survey in less than ⅓ of the median completion time of all participants in the same condition.
Participants who failed the attention check about the goals of the charities will be excluded from all analyses involving donations to those charities. Participants who fail the attention check embedded in the policy support questions will be excluded from all analyses of policy support. Participants who report identifying as an immigrant or transgender will be excluded. For longitudinal analyses, the baseline/immediate-wave exclusion rules specified in AsPredicted 288,741 will remain in force for the earlier-wave measures used in those analyses. Missing follow-up data will not be imputed. 		Wave-4 exclusions: failing two or more of the four instructed-response attention checks embedded in the survey (a skipped check counts as failed); duplicate submissions (first complete submission retained); as in Parts 1-2, a mixed or low result on either of Prolific’s authenticity checks (bot detection; AI/LLM detection), with a missing result not counting as a failure. Incomplete surveys are analyzed per measure: each analysis includes all participants with data on that measure and its covariates. In the longitudinal (multi-wave) models, observations from earlier waves are included only if they met that wave’s preregistered inclusion criteria. No outlier removal will be applied to any DV. We will exclude all participants who failed one or more of the four attention checks at the end of the study, and all participants who did not complete those attention checks.

We will additionally exclude participants who claim to have considered more than twice as many consequences of any given type in their second decision as the total number of consequences they wrote down for that decision. 		We will exclude all participants who failed one or more of the four attention checks at the end of the study, and all participants who did not complete those attention checks. We will exclude all participants who failed one or more of the four attention checks at the end of the study, and all participants who did not complete those attention checks.
sample_size We will run the experiment until each experimental condition has been completed by 600 participants who have passed the attention checks. This does not include participants who returned or exited the HIT before completing it. We aim to recruit 600 participants per condition (2400 participants in total). This does not include participants who return or abort the HIT before completing it. But it does include participants who complete the study but don’t meet the inclusion criteria. Recruitment will stop when 2400 participants have completed the study, or 14 days after launch, whichever comes first. If fewer than 2400 participants are recruited within this period, analyses will proceed with the obtained sample size. This follow-up HIT will be open to people who completed the initial HIT described in AsPredicted 288,741 (Stage 1) and didn’t meet its exclusion criteria.
We will recruit 600 participants per condition in Stage 1 (2400 participants in total). This does not include participants who return or abort the HIT before completing it. But it does include participants who complete the study but do not meet the inclusion criteria.
Stage 2 invitations will be issued on a rolling basis. Each day of the Stage 2 invitation period, participants who completed Stage 1 7 days prior without being excluded will be added to the Stage 2 participant group, making them eligible to begin Stage 2. Stage 2 data collection will close 7 days after the final cohort of Stage 1 completers is added to the Stage 2 participant group.
Assuming approximately 80% retention at follow-up, this recruitment target is intended to yield approximately 400 usable participants per condition for the longitudinal analyses after follow-up attrition and analytic exclusions. We will not recruit replacement participants to offset follow-up attrition.
