How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Levi Baruch

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Statement of Limitations

This research has several limitations. First, because the pathway from the intervention to more outgroup-prosocial choices was established through mediation analyses rather than by directly manipulating the mediators, the proposed mechanism of change is inferred rather than demonstrated. This caveat extends to the exploratory analyses in which we adjust the effect of SMR for post-intervention measures of participants' feelings and beliefs about the outgroup: because those measures are themselves affected by the intervention, adjusting for them bounds what the mechanism could be rather than identifying it. Second, some outcomes were measured with single items, and four of the five experiments measured generosity through hypothetical rather than real decisions. Third, the effects of our intervention were small to moderate in size and its effect on real donations faded substantially within 7 days. Therefore, the practical magnitude and durability of the benefits of performing systematic moral reflection once are modest and partly unknown. In the four-month follow-up of Experiment 3, which we designed after the initial submission, 44% of the invited participants did not return, and only two of five primary predictions were supported, both with small effects close to the threshold of statistical significance: an effect on donations, and the persistence of reduced dehumanization of immigrants, which was significant only when the observations of participants who did not return were included. We did not measure how much the size and durability of these benefits increase when the intervention is administered repeatedly. A further limitation concerns the thematic analysis of participants' written lessons: the lessons were parsed, coded, and clustered by large language models rather than by human coders, and the resulting theme counts were used as mediators. We validated this automated coding against independent human coders (see Sections S1.1.2-S1.1.7 of the Supplemental Materials). Finally, all participants were English-speaking adults in the United States responding in brief online studies, and the groups they considered were largely vulnerable people in need; whether the intervention works in other countries and languages, for other kinds of outgroups, for other types of consequential real-experiments are available in the project's OSF repository at https://osf.io/kgx7e/overview? view_only=cc06b98b9dab4ec6b04da6d085565570. All experiments were approved by the UCLA Office of the Human Research Protection Program (protocols IRB-25-1758 and IRB-25-2643). Generative AI tools were used at several stages of this research. The images that illustrated the social dilemmas in Experiments 1, S1, 2, and S2 were generated with DALL-E 3 (OpenAI, 2023). Claude Opus (Anthropic, 2025 (Anthropic, -2026) ) and ChatGPT (OpenAI, 2025 -2026) assisted with implementing the Qualtrics surveys and with the analysis code. The analysis code was written primarily by the researchers; Claude Opus wrote some of the data-cleaning functions and some of the analysis code, and ChatGPT was used to debug individual chunks of the analysis code for Experiments 1, S1, 2, and S2. All analysis scripts are available in the project's OSF repository. ChatGPT was also used to derive the sample size for Experiment 3 from parameters we specified, as described in that experiment's Participants section. Thinking (OpenAI, 2025) and the AIxGEO custom GPT (Turobov et al., 2024) were used to conduct the automatic thematic analysis of participants' written lessons, as detailed in Section S1.1.1 of the Supplemental Materials. Generative AI was also used to proofread and copy-edit the manuscript, to apply APA style, to check reported statistics against the analysis output, and to draft portions of the Results and Supplemental Materials reporting the exploratory analyses; all AI-generated text was reviewed and revised by the authors. No AI system contributed to the interpretation of the findings or to the conclusions drawn from them, and the authors verified the AI-assisted output and take responsibility for its accuracy.

Learning to Care More About Distant Others: A Systematic Moral Reflection Intervention

Many of the people who need help most receive the least. The welfare of the poor, the sick, and the marginalized depends on better-off strangers, whose attention, concern, and resources are captured almost entirely by their own families, friends, and communities (Balliet et al., 2014; Brewer, 1999; Jones & Rachlin, 2006). One reason is social categorization: people readily perceive those in greatest need as outgroup members, and the needs of outgroups, such as immigrants and transgender people, attract less attention and receive less weight in their decisions than comparable ingroup needs (Cikara et al., 2011; Levine et al., 2005; Nguyen et al., 2026). This parochial altruism (Bernhard et al., 2006; Choi & Bowles, 2007) skews the allocation of human generosity: most help makes already highly privileged people slightly better off while even the most basic needs of millions of people go almost completely unmet (Caviola et al., 2021).

The same ingroup-outgroup psychology that biases whom people help also breeds prejudice against outgroup members (Devine, 1989) and, at its worst, open intergroup hostility.

Classic research on intergroup conflict found that people's behavior toward an outgroup can be durably improved by cooperative intergroup contact (Allport, 1954; Mousa, 2020; Sherif, 1966; Woodley et al., 2025), and by perspective taking (e.g., Batson, 1997; Cialdini et al., 1997; Galinsky & Moskowitz, 2000; Neuberg et al., 1997). Both are assumed to work by improving attitudes toward the outgroup (e.g., reducing prejudice and dehumanization) and increasing perceived similarity, connectedness, and interpersonal warmth (Pettigrew & Tropp, 2008; Todd & Galinsky, 2014). Similarly, training people to cultivate compassion can increase costly helping toward suffering strangers (Weng et al., 2013). However, rigorous field experiments suggest that the effects of such prejudice-reduction interventions are limited in size, scope, and duration (Mousa, 2020; Paluck et al., 2021). Moreover, inducing empathy for one outgroup can cause preferential treatment of that outgroup at the expense of other outgroups and the greater good (Batson, Batson, et al., 1995; Batson, Klein, et al., 1995).

Here, we investigate a complementary mechanism: learning to make social decisions more considerately. Unlike interventions that target people's feelings about a particular group, this approach can transfer across situations and outgroups, without the favoritism empathy induction can produce (Batson, Batson, et al., 1995; Batson, Klein, et al., 1995). Recent work suggests that learning to decide more considerately may be partly driven by strategy selection learning (Maier et al., 2026): learning when to use which cognitive strategy by trying out candidate strategies and learning from how the resulting decisions turn out (Lieder & Griffiths, 2017). In this article, we apply this idea to social dilemmas, situations that pit the interests of the individual or their ingroup against the interests of (distant) others (Dawes, 1980; Van Lange et al., 2013).

Consistent with the idea that strategy selection learning shapes how people make social decisions, Rand et al. (2014) argued that when people forego their own self-interest in a social dilemma to benefit others, it is because they rely on social heuristics they learned in social environments where cooperating with others pays off (Peysakhovich & Rand, 2016). Similarly, Maier et al. (2026) found that when people repeatedly make decisions that affect others, they learn to select the strategies that have been most beneficial to the greater good in their experience. They call this mechanism metacognitive moral learning because it produces lasting, experience-driven changes in how people regulate their moral decision-making process (see Figure 1). This includes learning when to override moral rules (e.g., "Do no harm") by utilitarian reasoning. Their computational model formalizes this as reinforcement learning over decision mechanisms: judging that a previous decision was morally right reinforces the mechanism that produced it, whereas judging it morally wrong makes that mechanism less likely to be used again. Critically, those judgments are informed by the decision's consequences. Supporting this account, Maier et al. (2026) found that participants learned to follow moral rules or to override them with cost-benefit reasoning depending on which strategy produced better outcomes in the scenarios they encountered. Beyond shaping which decision strategy people select, learning from the consequences of past decisions can also adjust a second, distinct target: how much weight people assign to each stakeholder's welfare when they decide (moral weight learning; Spiteri, Maier, et al., 2026). Through this mechanism, realizing that you harmed distant others because you did not give them enough consideration can lead you to up-weight their welfare in subsequent decisions. This mechanism operates on the decision weights that strategies like cost-benefit reasoning draw on. Although these theorized mechanisms could make people more prosocial toward outgroups (outgroup prosociality), this hypothesis has not yet been tested empirically.

Moral learning in social dilemmas is difficult by default. People learn significantly less from their decisions' consequences for others than from the consequences for themselves (Rhoads et al., 2025). Moreover, a single social dilemma decision has direct and indirect, as well as immediate and long-term effects on many different stakeholders. Decision-makers reliably encounter the immediate consequences for themselves and for people close to them, but they rarely experience the delayed consequences for distant others. For instance, people splitting their savings between gifts for their children and donations for global development will experience most of the benefits of the former but (almost) none of the latter. This can bias their evaluation of whether they made the right choice and their takeaways for future decisions.

People's limited attention further exacerbates this bias (Spiteri, Gao, et al., 2026).

In this article, we develop and test Systematic Moral Reflection (SMR), a novel intervention designed to promote moral weight learning alongside strategy selection learning: it teaches people to adopt more deliberate decision strategies and to place greater weight on the welfare of distant others. Figure 1 depicts this learning loop and where SMR intervenes. A decision strategy produces a decision, the decision produces consequences, and the decisionmaker observes some of those consequences, attends to and processes a subset of them, and evaluates the decision in their light. The resulting evaluation reinforces or weakens whatever the learner holds in mind as the cause of the outcome. By default, that is the action itself. Thus, a decision-maker who never reflects on how they decided will rarely learn better decision strategies because learning cannot reinforce a cause that is not mentally represented. SMR intervenes at two points where this process can fall short. At the evaluation stage, it directs attention to the people the decision affected most, asks the decision-maker to take their perspective and consider how they would judge the decision, and asks whether a better decision was available. This helps people learn from how their decision affected distant others.

At the learning stage, it directs people to derive a general lesson about their decision process. This requires deliberate effort that people rarely make unprompted. The Socratic questions for this stage ask how the decision was made, whether a better strategy was available, and how to decide differently in the future. This helps people learn useful general lessons about how to make better decisions. In this way, SMR improves the learning signal and channels it into deeper, more generalizable change.

The obstacles described above are analogous to the difficulty of learning from delayed consequences, which prevents people from discovering far-sighted decision strategies. Becker et al. (2023) found that people can overcome these challenges by reflecting systematically: they were significantly more likely to discover far-sighted strategies when Socratic questions directed their attention to how far ahead they had planned and how well their decisions turned out.

Systematic reflection also boosts learning from experience in many other domains (Ellis et al., 2014). SMR is the first intervention to adapt this evidence-based approach to prosocial decisionmaking, asking people Socratic questions about their previous social decisions (see Table 2). Its Socratic questioning connects SMR to the Kohlbergian tradition of fostering moral learning through the discussion of moral dilemmas (Blatt & Kohlberg, 1975; Kohlberg & Hersh, 1977), but it directs that questioning at the consequences of a decision the person actually made, and at how they made it rather than at how they justify it.

This article tests whether properly facilitated metacognitive moral learning can increase outgroup prosociality. We test seven predictions of this account in five preregistered experiments (see Table 5). Our theory's first and most fundamental prediction is that guiding people to systematically reflect on a past intergroup decision makes their subsequent behavior toward outgroups more prosocial. All five experiments test this prediction, using consequential decisions. Because SMR incorporates perspective taking and evokes empathy for outgroup members, alternative accounts also predict such an effect. The remaining six predictions are distinctive: they follow from the claim that SMR changes how people decide, and attitudechange accounts either do not make them or predict otherwise.

The second prediction follows from what drives metacognitive moral learning. Because the loop in Figure 1 is driven by the decision-maker's evaluation of their own past decision, the more negatively participants evaluate their first decision, the more their subsequent intergroup behavior should change. Accounts on which reflection primes prosocial norms make no such prediction.

The third prediction is that SMR will change the decision process people use to make choices in intergroup dilemmas. Concretely, we predict that SMR will teach people to make intergroup decisions more deliberately, pay more attention to how their choices might affect distant others, and give those consequences more weight in future decisions. Moreover, the lessons participants formulate should predict subsequent changes in their behavior toward outgroups. We test this prediction by analyzing participants' written lessons and their considerations in subsequent intergroup decisions.

The fourth prediction concerns the mediators of SMR's effects on outgroup prosociality.

If SMR works by teaching people to decide differently, then the resulting changes in their decision process, including what consequences they attend to and how much weight they place on the outgroup's welfare, should partially mediate the effect of SMR on outgroup prosociality.

Moreover, because SMR teaches general strategies for social decision-making, rather than group-specific attitudes, its effects should not be reducible to the mechanisms of perspectivetaking and empathy-induction interventions, such as increased oneness with the outgroup (Galinsky & Moskowitz, 2000) or changes in people's beliefs and feelings about the outgroup.

Experiment 3 tests these predictions, using real donations.

The fifth prediction is transfer across multiple outgroups and behaviors. Because moral learning updates decision procedures and the weight attached to distant others in general rather than attitudes toward a particular group, its benefits should extend to groups the intervention never mentioned. Experiments 2 and 3 test this prediction, and Experiment S2 probes its boundary.

The sixth prediction concerns SMR's relationship to its ingredients. Because metacognitive moral learning adds guided deliberation about one's own past decision, and the derivation of an explicit lesson from it, to perspective taking and empathy induction, SMR should increase helping behavior toward the outgroup more than either of those interventions alone.

Experiment 3 tests this prediction by comparing SMR against both.

The seventh prediction is that, because the effects of SMR reflect learning rather than transient emotional states, the increase in helping behavior should persist for at least a week.

Experiment 3 tests this prediction as well.

Experiment 1: SMR Increases Outgroup Prosociality in Volunteering Decisions

Throughout this article, we examine the effects of SMR on a past social-dilemma decision, in which participants, in the role of a protagonist, had to weigh a small benefit for themselves and their ingroup against a large benefit for a distant outgroup in greater need. We predicted that SMR would teach people to prioritize the outgroup's needs more strongly in analogous future decisions. Experiment 1 provides the first and most direct test of this hypothesis. After seeing the outcomes of their first decision, half of the participants were guided to systematically reflect on it, and then all of them made a second, comparable decision.

Experiment 1 served as a proof of concept in a volunteering dilemma, which weighed the needs of an impoverished outgroup against a person's own time and their family. A preregistered replication and extension of this experiment in a donation dilemma (Experiment S1) is summarized at the end of this section and reported in full in Section S3 of the Supplemental Materials.

Methods

Participants

We recruited English-speaking adults located in the United States via Prolific. We posted the experimental (SMR) and control conditions as two mutually exclusive Prolific HITs with the same name and description; once a participant accepted either HIT, they were redirected to the corresponding Qualtrics survey and became ineligible for the other. As preregistered, we continued data collection until 255 participants had completed each condition. We chose this sample size to achieve 80% power to detect a small effect on the second decision, after accounting for attention-check exclusions and the reduced efficiency of the nonparametric ANCOVA used as the primary analysis. The retained sample of 435 participants, together with the observed correlation of r = .49 between the first and second decisions, provides 80% power to detect an effect of d = 0.21 in a one-tailed test at α = .05. Each group received a bonus that increased its median wage to $9.50 per hour. We excluded participants who failed any preregistered attention checks before analysis; retention was comparable across conditions.

Table 1 summarizes sample sizes, demographics, and compensation.

Experimental Design

This experiment used a between-subjects design with two conditions: SMR and control.

Both conditions completed the same two social dilemma decisions (described below); the SMR condition additionally completed a systematic moral reflection exercise between the two decisions. The second decision served as the primary, preregistered measure of outgroup prosociality.

Materials and Measures

Social Dilemmas

The experiment presented two social dilemmas embedded in a single cover story, in which the participant took the role of a protagonist who repeatedly faced a conflict between the welfare of a distant, impoverished outgroup and the interests of an ingroup and themselves. The two dilemmas were matched in structure but differed in surface details so that the second decision was clearly distinct from the first. After the first decision, all participants were shown the outcome of their decision for both groups.

Participants took the role of one of two full-time employees of a food-assistance nonprofit serving people in need in the local community. In each dilemma, the protagonist could allocate their free time between volunteering unpaid hours to help the short-staffed organization meet its target of feeding people in need and being with their family (Decision 1) or earning money for a future family vacation (Decision 2). Participants indicated how many hours they would volunteer on a slider ranging from 0 to 20 hours in 1-hour increments. Each social dilemma was illustrated with images generated with DALL-E 3 (OpenAI, 2023) from prompts describing the scenario depicted in the corresponding vignette. The scenarios and response scales are described below; the full vignettes are reproduced in Section S5.1 of the Supplemental Materials.

Systematic Moral Reflection Intervention

The systematic moral reflection intervention was implemented as a series of Socratic questions within the experiment's Qualtrics survey. As illustrated in Figure 1, the Socratic questions guided participants to evaluate the first decision from the perspectives of everyone who was affected by it (Steps 1-5), analyze how they reached that decision (Step 6), identify how this decision process could be improved (Steps 7-8), and plan to apply those improvements to future decisions (Step 9). For a more detailed description of the nine steps, see Table 2. The full text of every question is available in the experiment's Qualtrics survey in the project's online repository.

Table 2

Steps of the Systematic Moral Reflection Intervention

Step Description 1 Describing the decision process

Participants describe how they reached their decision by answering three questions about i) how long they thought about the decision, ii) how much of that time they had spent on thinking about consequences for themselves, and iii) how much of that time they had spent on thinking about consequences for others. Participants then rate how important considerations about consequences for i) themselves, ii) people they know, and iii) people they don't know personally were for their decision.

After that, they rate how much they had relied on each of the ten decision strategies listed in Table A1 in Appendix A. Those decision strategies range from non-moral strategies, such as "I have chosen this action many times before" and "I followed my gut feeling", to "I followed the norms, rules, or expectations of my community," and explicitly moral strategies, such as "I followed my moral intuitions about right and wrong", "I wanted to ensure fairness", and "I wanted to maximize the sum of everyone's wellbeing." 2 Evaluating the most important consequences

Participants systematically reflect on the outcomes of their decision. They are reminded of the outcome of their decision and then on who was most positively/negatively affected by their decision, put themselves into those people's shoes as they experienced the consequences, and then rate how bad it was for one person to experience those consequences and how bad it was for all affected individuals to experience those consequences. Participants first complete this process for the group or individual who was most positively affected by their decision, and then complete it for the group or individual who was most negatively affected by their decision. To help participants overcome omission bias, we ask them to consider the effect of having chosen what they chose over the alternative that would have been worst/best for others. 3 Taking an outside perspective.

Participants judge how good and how selfish they were from the perspectives of the people who were most positively affected and from the perspective of the people who were most negatively affected by their decision. 4 Attending to (moral) emotions Participants report how good or how bad they felt about their decision.

5 Evaluating the decision

Participants evaluate the quality of their decision from a moral perspective. 6 Auditing the decision process for potential mistakes

Participants critically examine the process through which they reached the decision with an eye to how it could be improved. The questions specifically direct their attention to whether they had given the decision enough thought and whether they had paid enough attention to the decision's potential consequences for all stakeholders. Participants then rate their overall satisfaction with the decision process. 7 Finding a better decision Participants reconsidered whether they could have made a better decision and what it might have been.

Step Description 8 Finding a better decision strategy

Participants determine what change in their decision process could make it more likely that they would reach this better decision in similar situations in the future. The questions specifically direct them to consider whether it would have been helpful to i) pay less attention to consequences for themselves, ii) pay more attention to consequences for friends and family, ii) pay more attention to consequences for other people they know, iv) pay more attention to consequences for other people they don't know, v) follow the Golden Rule, vi) ensure fairness, vii) follow their moral intuitions about right and wrong, viii) maximize the sum of everyone's well-being, and four other decision strategies. 9 Formulating implementation intentions

Participants formulated an If-Then plan for when to apply this improved decision strategy in similar situations in the future.

Measures of the Decision Process

After the second decision, participants answered a series of questions about how they had made it. These questions covered the consequences they considered in making their decision and their decision strategies.

Considered consequences. Participants were first asked to list all potential consequences they had considered. Participants then counted how many of the consequences they had listed affected each of four stakeholder groups (themselves, friends and family, acquaintances, and strangers) and rated how important the outcomes for each group had been for their decision on a 7-point scale (with an additional "I didn't think of any such consequences" option scored as 0).

Decision strategies.

We asked participants to rate how much each of the ten decision strategies listed in Table A1 in Appendix A contributed to their choice on a 6-point scale ranging from not at all to more than anything else. One of those strategies was utilitarian cost-benefit reasoning ("I wanted to maximize the sum of everyone's wellbeing."). Participants could also list additional considerations that were important to them.

Appraisals of the First Decision (SMR Condition Only)

During the reflection, participants in the SMR condition rated how they felt about their first decision on a bipolar scale from -100 (extremely negatively) to +100 (extremely positively) after analyzing its consequences. Later during the reflection, they rated their satisfaction with the thought process that led to this decision on a bipolar scale from -100 (completely dissatisfied) to +100 (completely satisfied).

Reliability

All measures in Experiment 1 were single-item or behavioral; the experiment included no multi-item composite scale.

Procedure

After providing informed consent, participants read the cover story described above and made a first decision. Immediately afterward, all participants were shown the outcome of that decision for all affected parties, including both the outgroup and the protagonist's ingroup.

Participants in the SMR condition then completed the systematic moral reflection intervention; while participants in the control condition proceeded directly to the next stage.

After a short break, all participants made a second decision later in the same story and reported how they reached it. Finally, all participants completed four true/false attention-check questions assessing their comprehension of the scenario, and provided feedback on the study.

Data Analysis

Analyses followed the preregistrations and were conducted in R. Exploratory analyses are labeled as such throughout. We tested the effect of SMR on outgroup prosociality in the second decision using the non-parametric ANCOVA function from the R package sm with an automatically chosen smoothing kernel width (Bowman & Azzalini, 1997). We probed the underlying mechanisms with two converging approaches: First, preregistered causal mediation analyses (R package mediation; Tingley et al., 2014; 10,000 bootstrap simulations) tested whether SMR's effect on the second decision was mediated by specified changes in the decision process, controlling for the first decision. Second, we conducted an automatic thematic analysis of the free-text lessons that the SMR-condition participants wrote at the end of their reflection. In brief, we parsed each response into atomic behavioral and metacognitive lessons, coded them for themes, clustered them into a small set of higher-order themes, and then counted how many themes from each cluster appeared in each participant's lesson (see Section S1.1.1 for the full procedure and prompts). Exploratory parallel multiple-mediation models (R package lavaan 0.6-19; Rosseel, 2012) tested whether these theme counts mediated the effect of SMR on the second decision, with one mediator per theme cluster (set to zero for controlcondition participants) and the first decision included as a covariate. To assess the reliability of the automatic cluster assignments, two independent human coders re-coded all 205 lessons against the fixed cluster definitions. Critically, both coders were blind to the AI-generated labels and the mediation results. Within the analytic sample, their codes reproduced the automatic assignments well for every cluster that carried a significant indirect effect (mean human-model κ = .63-.91; inter-coder κ = .88-1.00), supporting the reliability of the mediator measurements (see Sections S1.1.2 and S1.1.3). Nonparametric procedures have no degrees of freedom: the nonparametric ANCOVAs, the bootstrapped t tests, and the z tests reported for Kendall's τ and for indirect effects are therefore reported without them. Degrees of freedom are reported for all parametric tests.

Results

SMR Increased Outgroup Prosociality in the Following Decision

Consistent with Prediction 1, systematic moral reflection significantly increased outgroup prosociality in the second decision. Participants who reflected on their first decision subsequently volunteered about 2.4 hours more (M = 9.5, SD = 6.1) than control participants (M = 7.1, SD = 5.3); the preregistered nonparametric ANCOVA testing equality of the two conditions' regression curves (smoothing bandwidth h = 1.61) confirmed that the difference was statistically significant, p < .001, as did a t test, t(426.58) = 4.44, p < .001, d = 0.43, 95% CI [0.24, 0.62]; see Figure 2A. Full descriptive statistics for the measures in this experiment are reported in Table S2.3.1.

A second nonparametric ANCOVA tested whether the two conditions' regression curves were parallel, that is, whether the effect was constant across levels of the first decision (see Figure S2.7.1 in the Supplemental Materials). Reflection raised volunteering uniformly, with no evidence against parallel regression curves (h = 1.61, p > .999).

Which Lessons Did Participants Learn Through Systematic Moral Reflection?

About 81% of participants in the SMR condition reported learning a useful lesson (81.3%). An automatic thematic analysis identified six clusters of lessons among these participants (n = 178; see Table S2.1.1). The most common cluster, present in 59.0% of the lessons, expressed a resolve to prioritize the greater good, strangers, and those in greatest need. In addition, 38.2% of the lessons expressed a commitment to act prosocially (e.g., to volunteer when needed), 28.1% resolved to decide more deliberately by taking time, gathering information, and weighing trade-offs, and 19.7% invoked ethical principles, empathy, or perspective-taking. Two smaller clusters ran counter to the outgroup-prosocial direction of the majority: 15.7% of the lessons resolved to set boundaries and protect one's own time and energy, and 7.9% resolved to prioritize or consult one's family. For the complete definitions of the six clusters, example lessons, and their frequencies, see Section S2.1.1. Note. (A) Hours volunteered in Experiment 1 and (B) proportion of extra profits donated in Experiment S1, both measuring the effect on a subsequent decision in the same domain as the first decision. (C) Proportion of extra profits donated in Experiment 2, measuring transfer from a volunteering decision to a donation decision benefiting a different outgroup. (D) Proportion of extra profits donated in Experiment S2, measuring transfer to a dilemma in which both the outgroup and the ingroup differed from those of the first decision. Points are individual participants, jittered to reduce overplotting; shaded shapes are kernel density estimates; markers and error bars show condition means with 95% confidence intervals.

Experiments S1 and S2 are reported in full in Sections S3 and S4 of the Supplemental Materials. SMR = systematic moral reflection.

The Lessons Participants Learned Mediated the Effect

Consistent with Predictions 3 and 4, the content of participants' written lessons accounted for most of the effect of systematic moral reflection on the second decision (71.0% mediated, p = .015). The presence of themes about prioritizing the greater good or strangers who are worse off mediated 31.7% of the effect. In addition, lessons expressing a commitment to act prosocially mediated 24.0% of the effect, and resolving to decide more deliberately mediated 18.3%. One theme worked against the effect: resolving to prioritize or consult one's family had a significant negative indirect effect (p = .002). Table S2.2.1 provides the statistical details.

Corresponding Changes in the Decision Process Mediated the Effect

Further supporting Predictions 3 and 4, reflection produced parallel shifts in how participants made the second decision, and these shifts mediated the increase in outgroup prosociality. Reflection on the first decision reduced participants' tendency to prioritize consequences affecting their ingroup over consequences affecting the outgroup in the second decision: SMR decreased the importance participants placed on consequences for friends and family (from M = 4.43 out of 7 in the control condition to M = 3.82 after SMR; t(432.5) = -3.14, p < .001), and this mediated about 17.1% of SMR's effect on outgroup prosociality (p = .002).

SMR also taught people to deliberate more: compared with the control group, the SMR group invested more time in the second decision (M = 114.95 s vs. 92.26 s; Welch's t test on log-transformed response times, t(432.87) = 2.76, p = .003, d = 0.27) and relied less on habits (M = 2.24 vs. 2.54, t(428.24) = 2.29, p = .011, d = 0.22), gut feelings (M = 3.41 vs. 3.65, t(404.10) = 2.11, p = .018, d = 0.20), and social norms (M = 2.12 vs. 2.36, t(432.88) = 1.72, p = .043, d = 0.17; all ps one-tailed, as preregistered), with no change in reliance on moral intuitions (M = 3.43 vs. 3.50, p = .269). Notably, participants' stated reliance on utilitarian reasoning did not mediate the effect of SMR (6.3% mediated, p = .235, 95% CI [-4.6%, 19.0%]), indicating that the effect does not reduce to making people more calculating.

The full results of these causal mediation analyses are reported in Table S2.6.1.

How Systematic Moral Reflection Promoted Prosocial Learning

Consistent with the account's second prediction, the affective and evaluative consequences of the first decision predicted how much participants' behavior changed.

The worse participants felt about their first decision, the more their second decision shifted toward helping the outgroup (Kendall's τ = -.30foot_0 , p < .001). Likewise, greater dissatisfaction with the strategy behind the first decision predicted a larger subsequent shift (τ = -.31, p < .001). This pattern is consistent with participants' negative evaluations of their first decision shifting them away from the strategy that produced it.

Replication and Extension in a Donation Dilemma (Experiment S1)

To test whether SMR can promote prosocial behaviors beyond volunteering, we conducted a preregistered replication and extension of Experiment 1 with a donation decision (Experiment S1; N = 434, d = 0.31; see Figure 2B and Section S3 of the Supplemental Materials for the full report). Participants took the role of a CEO deciding how much of an unexpected profit to donate to a highly effective charity versus return to their employees. SMR increased the proportion of the available profit donated in the second decision from 43.1% to 51.9% (d = 0.31, p = .001), even though the second decision involved a different charity serving a different cause.

About 81% of reflecting participants reported learning a useful lesson. The content of those lessons-especially resolving to prioritize the most vulnerable people in greatest need-fully mediated the effect (67.1% mediated, p = .025). A finer-grained, consequence-level measure of the decision process showed that SMR led participants to consider more consequences for the outgroup and to assign them greater importance relative to consequences for the ingroup; these attentional changes mediated substantial portions of the effect (21.5% and 36.6%, respectively).

Notably, SMR had no effect on moral universalism (Enke et al., 2022), that is, on how participants allocated money between more and less socially distant strangers in the absence of any information about their needs (all p ≥ .55). The lessons participants learned thus generalized to a new decision benefiting similar people in need but not to impartiality toward strangers in general. Experiment S1 thereby replicated the support for Predictions 1, 2, 3, and 4 in a new decision domain (see Section S3).

Discussion

In Experiment 1 and its preregistered replication in a donation dilemma (Experiment S1), a single round of systematic moral reflection increased participants' outgroup prosociality in the subsequent decision. Retrospective self-reports about the decision process and thematic analysis of participants' written lessons converged on the same account: SMR taught participants to deliberate more, to give more weight to potential consequences for distant others, and to place correspondingly less weight on the comparatively minor consequences for themselves and their ingroup. Those lessons mediated most of the effect.

These results fit the view that moral decision-making is shaped by metacognitive reinforcement learning (Maier et al., 2026; Spiteri, Maier, et al., 2026): a negative verdict on a previous decision shifts one away from the strategy that produced it. Feeling bad about the first decision, and dissatisfied with the strategy behind it, predicted how much the second decision shifted toward helping the outgroup (Prediction 2). The change was not confined to repeating the same behavior: in Experiment S1, reflecting on a donation to one charity increased giving to a second charity addressing a different cause, suggesting that participants learned a more deliberate, more other-regarding way of deciding rather than a particular behavior.

The change also cannot be reduced to utilitarian cost-benefit reasoning: at least some participants appear to have learned to prioritize those in greatest need. Consistent with this, SMR increased giving to people in need and to African children at risk of dying from preventable disease, without changing how participants split money between more and less distant others when they have no information about those recipients' needs (see Section S3).

The following experiments address three limitations of these studies: whether the effects of SMR transfer to other social groups, whether they extend to real, consequential donations, and whether SMR changed behavior by changing feelings and beliefs about the outgroup, as prejudice-reduction interventions are thought to do.

Experiment 2: When Do the Benefits of Systematic Moral Reflection Transfer to Other Usvs-Them Dilemmas Involving Other Social Groups? Experiment 1 and its supplemental replication (Experiment S1) established that systematic moral reflection can increase outgroup prosociality in volunteering and donation decisions. However, in each experiment, the outgroup benefiting from the second decision was the same as the outgroup in the first decision (food-insecure Americans in Experiment 1, children in Africa in Experiment S1). Therefore, Experiment 2 tested whether the increase in outgroup prosociality transfers to another outgroup (sick children receiving surgery) and from one type of decision (volunteering) to another (donating).

Methods

In this preregistered experiment, all participants made a volunteering decision, then either went through the systematic moral reflection process (experimental condition) or not (control condition), and finally made a donation decision and completed additional measures.

Participants

We recruited 1,547 participants on Prolific Academic. All participants were Englishspeaking adults located in the USA. Their ages ranged from 18 to 83 (M = 38.7, SD = 13.24). Of participants with reported gender, 53.1% were female. Of the participants with reported ethnicity, 64.4% identified as White, 25.1% as Black, 2.1% as Asian, 5.2% as Mixed, and 3.3% as Other.

Before the main data collection, we decided to reduce the number of attention checks from the four checks mentioned in the pre-registration to two attention checks (one per scenario). Our rationale was to keep the exclusion rate near 25%, whereas the preregistered criterion would have excluded 37% of participants. About 77% of the recruited participants (1,195 people) passed both selected attention checks and were included in the subsequent analyses. These participants were evenly distributed across both conditions (596 in the control condition and 599 in the experimental condition). A sensitivity analysis confirmed that this deviation did not affect our conclusions (see Table S2.10.1 in the Supplemental Materials)foot_1.

Procedure

The procedure for recruiting participants and assigning them to the experimental condition versus the control condition was the same as in the previous experiments.
