As preregistered, we aimed to continue data collection until 600 participants in each condition had passed the attention checks; after final data cleaning, slightly fewer analyzable participants remained (596 and 599; see Participants). Because the effect of interest in this experiment is transfer to a second dilemma involving a different outgroup, we set this target to be sensitive to an effect smaller than the within-domain effects of Experiments 1 and S1. The attained sample size provides 80% power to detect an effect of d = 0.14 in a one-tailed test at α = .05.

In the experiment, participants first read a fictional scenario, asking them to put themselves in the shoes of an employee of a non-profit organization that provides food to people in need. They then decided how much of their free time to volunteer to help their organization achieve its goals. In this decision, participants had to trade off benefits to foodinsecure beneficiaries against costs to themselves and their family and friends. Then, all participants were informed of the outcome of their decision and answered two attention-check questions. Next, as in Experiment 1, the participants in the experimental condition engaged in systematic moral reflection. Next, both groups read a second vignette, asking them to now put themselves in the shoes of an accountant who must decide how much of a holiday bonus check to donate to a local children's hospital or spend on a family vacation to Bali. In this decision, participants had to trade off the potential benefit to sick, underprivileged children against the benefit to themselves and their families. This second decision served as our preregistered measure of the transfer of the intervention's effect on prosociality toward a different outgroup.

Finally, all participants reported on how they reached their decision, answered additional questions measuring social discounting, moral expansiveness, and impartial beneficence, and completed two further attention checks and a comprehension-confirmation item.

Materials and Measures

Social Dilemmas

The first social dilemma pitted the needs of a large outgroup (i.e., food-insecure individuals) against the interests of a charity worker, their family, and their friends. Participants entered this dilemma as the charity worker. They first read a description of the charity's activities and the charity worker's general life circumstances and work environment (see Section S5.3.1).

The description specified that the charity provides meals to food-insecure people in the local community. It also specified that the charity worker has three children and splits the childcare responsibilities equally with their spouse. The scenario description also noted that the charity operates under tight constraints and is sometimes short-staffed. In this volunteering dilemma, each participant decided how many hours they would volunteer in the second week of April to help the charity achieve its goals despite the illness of a co-worker (see Section S5.3).

Participants reported their decision by dragging a slider over a range from 0 hours to 20 hours in 1-hour increments.

The second social dilemma (see Section S5.3.2) pitted the needs of a local children's hospital against the interests of an accountant and their family. In the shoes of an accountant who was awarded a $4,995 holiday bonus check for excellent work completed that year, participants had the choice to donate to the local children's hospital to help fund new surgical equipment or spend it on booking a family vacation to Bali. Participants again responded on a slider ranging from $0 donated (and everything to the family vacation) to $4,995 donated (and nothing to the family vacation).

Each social dilemma was illustrated with images generated with DALL-E 3 (OpenAI, 2023) from prompts describing the scenario depicted in the corresponding vignette. The authors selected the final images and used them without further modification.

Systematic Moral Reflection

The Socratic questions of the systematic reflection intervention comprised the same nine steps as in Experiments 1 and S1 (see Table 2). However, we made slight enhancements to was split up into a series of questions that guided participants to i) find a way of thinking (strategy) that can help them avoid the mistake they reflected on, ii) to identify future situations where this strategy can help them make better decisions, and iii) formulate an If-Then plan to apply the chosen strategy in the relevant situations. Participants then reported how soon they would implement the lesson and answered a series of Likert-scale questions about the usefulness of the lesson they created and their likelihood of implementing it in real life.

Measures of the Decision Process

Assessment of the considered consequences. Following the second decision, participants answered a series of questions about their decision process. To ground participants' ratings in what they actually considered during the decision process, we first asked them to list which potential outcomes they considered while making their second decision. We also presented participants with a list of morally significant potential consequences affecting the outgroup and asked them to indicate which they had considered. We then asked participants to rate the consequences on a series of dimensions. These questions were similar to those of Experiments 1 and S1, except that they also assessed the moral significance of self-generated consequences. Specifically, we measured the following factors for each considered consequence:

Importance. Participants rated how important each consequence had been for their decision on a scale from 0-100.

Extremity.

Participants rated how good or bad each consequence would be on a scale from -100 to 100. We calculated each consequence's extremity as the absolute value of this rating.

Stakeholder.

Participants chose which group the consequence affected.

Likelihood.

Participants rated the likelihood of the consequence by predicting how many times it would occur if they took the same action 100 times, on a 0-100 scale.

Number of people affected.

Participants entered a single number indicating how many people they thought the consequence would affect.

Change in well-being.

Participants rated the change in well-being by assessing how much happier or unhappier those affected would be, on a scale of -10 to 10.

Duration.

Participants indicated how long the change in well-being would last by completing a form field comprising a unit of time and a number.

Moral significance.

We calculated the moral significance of each reported consequence by multiplying its likelihood by the number of people affected, the well-being change per person affected, and the duration of this effect. Following Nguyen et al. (2026), we categorized a consequence as morally significant if this product was equal to or greater than 0.37 well-beingadjusted life years.

Assessment of the decision strategies.

We measured how much participants had relied on moral intuition, deliberation, moral rules, and cost-benefit reasoning when making the second decision using the 12-item Decision Process Scale developed by Cheung et al. (2026) on a scale from 0 (not at all) to 100 (entirely).

Participants also rated how much each of the four decision strategies listed in Table A2 in Appendix A contributed to their choice on a 6-point scale ranging from not at all to more than anything else. Participants could also list additional considerations that were important to them.

Moral Concern

Participants were presented with a list of individuals from various social groups, comprising a family member, a close friend, a coworker, a community member, an elderly community member, a special needs community member, a children's hospital patient in the community (the outgroup in the second decision), and a complete stranger. Participants were asked to place each individual in one of the four circles of moral concern from the moral expansiveness scale (Crimston et al., 2016)

Social Discounting

Participants completed the short-form of the social discounting task developed by Amormino et al. (2025). This task first asks participants to list the first, second, fifth, tenth, twentieth, and fiftieth closest people to them. Participants then make a series of seven allocation http://decisions.in/. In the first six allocation decisions, they allocated $10 between themselves and one of the individuals they had listed. In the last allocation decision, they allocated $10 between themselves and a member of the outgroup in the second decision.

Impartial Beneficence

Participants rated their agreement with the 5 statements of the impartial beneficence sub-scale of the Oxford Utilitarianism Scale (Kahane et al., 2018).

Reliability

Cronbach's α for the impartial beneficence sub-scale, computed on the present sample, was .81, and αs for the four decision-process subscales ranged from .71 to .79 (see Table S1.3 for further detail).

All other measures in this experiment were single-item or behavioral.

Attention Checks

The attention checks comprised four true-or-false questions about the two social dilemmas. The first two followed the first social dilemma decision, and the final two followed the measures asking participants how they had reached the second decision. A fifth true-or-false item, asking participants to confirm that the consequences they had listed referred only to the second decision, served as a comprehension confirmation and was not used for exclusions.

Data Analysis Plan

We preregistered the data analysis at https://aspredicted.org/cgd2-qp42.pdf. Social discounting was computed using a non-linear mixed effects model developed by Amormino et al. (2025), using the model formula "amount ~ v0/(1 + exp(logk) * social_distance)". We tested between-group differences in moral concern with bootstrapped t-tests using 10,000 resamples, which we report without degrees of freedom.

To statistically identify lesson types that promote outgroup prosociality, we conducted causal mediation analyses using bootstrapping with 10,000 simulations with the mediation package in R (Tingley et al., 2014). All mediation analyses controlled for individual differences in the first decision.

Results

Transfer Effects on Outgroup Prosociality and Social Discounting

Consistent with Predictions 1 and 5, the effect of systematic moral reflection on a volunteering decision transferred to donations supporting a different outgroup: systematic reflection significantly increased the amount donated to charity in the second decision relative to the control condition (h = 0.0851, p < .001; see Figure 2C and Figure S2.7.2 in the Supplemental Materials). After reflecting on the outcomes of the initial volunteering decision and how it would affect each group in question, participants in the experimental condition decided to donate $501.92 more to a local children's hospital than the control group: donations increased from $1,914.91 in the control condition (M = $1,914.91, SD = $1,302.31) to $2,416.83 in the experimental condition (M = $2,416.83, SD = $1,396.39; t(1188) = -6.43, p < .001; d = 0.37, 95% CI [0.26, 0.49]).

Transfer Effects on Other Measures of Prosociality Toward Distant Others

We found strong evidence of transfer effects to the three other measures of outgroup prosociality: social discounting, moral concern for distant others, and impartial beneficence.

Systematic moral reflection significantly increased participants' average level of moral concern for non-close others (i.e., the six targets other than their family member and their close friend; M = 1.70, SD = 0.57) compared to those who did not go through the reflection (M = 1.60, SD = 0.55; t = 3.23, p = .002; d = 0.19, 95% CI [0.07, 0.30]), including the sick children from the second dilemma (t = 3.50, p = .001; d = 0.20, 95% CI [0.09, 0.32]).

Systematic moral reflection also increased participants' endorsement of the moral principle of impartial beneficence (t(1190.6) = 3.25, p = .001; d = 0.19, 95% CI [0.07, 0.30]) from 4.01 out of 7 in the control group (SD = 1.34) to 4.26 out of 7 in the experimental group (SD = 1.29).

Moreover, the rate at which participants' discounting of the welfare of others increased with their social distance was significantly lower for participants who had engaged in systematic reflection (log(k) = -5.74) than for the control group (log(k) = -5.27), b = -0.47, SE = 0.12, t(7168) = -4.08, p < .001.

In an exploratory follow-up analysis, we asked whether these changes in participants' moral concern and endorsement of impartial beneficence could account for the increase in donations. We found that they could not fully account for it, although they accounted for part of it: adjusting simultaneously for moral concern for the sick children, moral concern for the six non-close targets, and impartial beneficence reduced the effect of systematic moral reflection on the amount donated from b = 502.91 to b = 375.31, which remained significant, t(1189) = 5.21, p < .001 (see Table S2.9.5). Because these measures were assessed after the second decision, this analysis bounds rather than identifies the mechanism, and because Experiment 2 included no measure of participants' feelings toward the outgroup, it speaks only to their beliefs.

Experiment 3 tests this question with a fuller set of measures.

What Lessons Did Participants Learn?

As part of the systematic moral reflection, participants rated how much different strategies could have helped them reach a better decision. The average participant reported that relying more on cost-benefit reasoning would have been more helpful than not (M = 1.82, SD = 1.23, t(598) = 36.27, p < .001), and that paying more attention to outcomes for people they don't know would be more helpful (M = 1.37, SD = 1.41) than paying more attention to outcomes for their friends and family (M = 0.89, SD = 1.45; paired t(598) = 6.17, p < .001; dz = 0.25, 95% CI [0.17, 0.33]).

Consistent with these findings, our automatic thematic analysis of the lessons participants wrote down at the end of their systematic moral reflection found that, of all participants in the experimental condition, 57.6% learned a lesson endorsing deliberative, evidence-based decision-making, 33.9% learned a lesson endorsing impartial beneficence or the prioritization of vulnerable others even if they are distant, 24.9% learned a lesson about prioritizing their family or setting boundaries to protect their own well-being, 22.0% resolved to involve other members of their organization/community in the decision or decide according to their preferences, 21.4% learned a lesson endorsing an ethical principle or moral constraint, and 6.2% of participants learned a lesson endorsing continuous improvement, learning from feedback, or reflection (see Table S2.1.3 for details).

Participants in the experimental group rated the lessons they wrote down at the end of the reflection as useful for making better decisions (M = 1.53 on a scale from -2 to +2, SD = 0.48, t(540) = 73.64, p < .001) and reported that they were likely to use them in similar social dilemmas (M = 1.58 on a scale from -2 to +2, SD = 0.55, t(543) = 66.38, p < .001).

The Lessons Participants Learned Partially Mediated the Effect of Systematic Moral Reflection on Outgroup Prosociality

Our parallel mediation analysis found that the presence of the six types of themes in the lessons participants wrote down (see Table S2.1.3) mediated about 38.5% (95% CI [10.1%, 79.1%]) of the effect of systematic moral reflection.

Among the potential mediators, only learning a lesson expressing the principle of impartial beneficence or giving priority to the vulnerable/distant had a significant indirect effect, B = 128.50, 95% CI [68.88, 193.07], z = 4.07, p < .001, corresponding to about 25.6% of the total effect of systematic moral reflection. For more details, see Section S2.2 and Table S2.2.3.

As in Experiment 1, two independent human coders, who were blind to the model's assignments and to the mediation results, re-coded a random sample of 150 lessons from the analytic sample against the fixed cluster definitions. Their codes reproduced the automatic assignments well for the cluster that carried the significant indirect effect (impartial beneficence; mean Cohen's κ = .64 against the LLM, with inter-coder agreement of κ = .79), supporting the reliability of the mediator measurement (see Sections S1.1.4 and S1.1.5 of the Supplemental Materials).

Learning-Induced Changes in People's Decision Strategies Increased Outgroup Prosociality

Analyzing how participants made the second decision revealed that systematic moral reflection induced several related changes in participants' decision strategies that contributed to the increase in outgroup prosociality. Several of these changes were consistent with people learning to give more consideration to vulnerable/distant others and become more impartial.

Mediation by self-reported decision strategies.

Consistent with Predictions 3 and 4, exploratory mediation analyses indicated that three types of strategies participants reported using in the second decision mediated a significant proportion of the reflection-induced increase in outgroup prosociality. In the second decision, the experimental group reported striving to behave like a virtuous person or moral saint to a greater extent (M = 3.38, SD = 1.47) than the control group (M = 3.03, SD = 1.61; t(1182.1) = -3.87, p < .001; d = 0.22, 95% CI [0.11, 0.34]). This mediated about 27.0% of the effect of systematic moral reflection on outgroup prosociality.

Similarly, the experimental group reported relying more on the Golden Rule ("Treat others the way you want to be treated"; M = 3.51, SD = 1.36) than the control group (M = 3.22, SD = 1.55; t(1172.4) = -3.46, p = .001; d = 0.20, 95% CI [0.09, 0.31]). This mediated about 23.7% of the effect of systematic reflection on outgroup prosociality.

Finally, the experimental group wanted to ensure fairness significantly more (M = 3.61, SD = 1.2) than the control group (M = 3.40, SD = 1.29; t(1185.8) = -2.84, p = .005; d = 0.16, 95% CI [0.05, 0.28]). This explained about 17.5% of the effect of systematic moral reflection on outgroup prosociality.

According to the Decision Process Scale, there was a significant decrease in reliance on moral intuition for those who went through the reflection (M = 71.67, SD = 18.69) compared to those who didn't (M = 73.99, SD = 17.83; t(1190.9) = -2.19, p = .029; d = -0.13, 95% CI [-0.24, -0.01]); however, it was not found to be a significant mediator to outgroup prosociality. Participants' self-reported reliance on deliberation, moral rules, and costbenefit reasoning was unaffected (all ps ≥ .100; see Table S2.11.1). For more details on these mediation analyses, see Table S2.6.3 in the Supplemental Materials.

Mediation by increased attention to outgroup consequences. Systematic reflection significantly increased the number of outgroup consequences participants considered in the second decision from 3.83 in the control group (SD = 2.39) to 4.43 in the experimental group (SD = 2.44; t(1192.7) = -4.24, p < .001; d = 0.25, 95% CI [0.13, 0.36]).

We found that this increase in participants' attention to outgroup consequences mediated 26.1% of the effect of the reflection on how much money they donated in the second decision (95% CI [15.1%, 40%], p < .001). The indirect effect of the reflection on money donated through an increase in considered outgroup consequences was $131.30 (95% CI [$70.01, $195.10], p < .001).

Moreover, as predicted, SMR also increased the number of morally significant consequences participants considered in the second decision from 5.59 in the control group (SD = 3.49) to 6.01 in the experimental group (SD = 3.46; t(1192.8) = -2.09, p = .037; d = 0.12, 95% CI [0.01, 0.23]). This increase mediated 13.1% of the effect of the reflection on how much money they donated to help the outgroup (95% CI [1.4%, 25.0%], p = .031; indirect effect = $66.04, 95% CI [$6.73, $128.13]; see Table S2.6.3 in the Supplemental Materials).

Mediation by the relative importance of consequences affecting the outgroup.

Systematic reflection significantly increased the relative importance the experimental group assigned to outgroup consequences (M = 7.33, SD = 22.31) relative to the control group (M = 3.71, SD = 20.68; t(860.99) = -2.48, p = .007), as measured by the difference between the importance ratings of the most important outgroup consequence and the most important ingroup consequence.

We found that 15.32% of the effect of systematic reflection on money donated to help the outgroup was mediated by an increase in the relative importance of the decision's effects on the outgroup (95% CI [3.47%, 28.73%], p = .011). The indirect effect on money donated through an increase in the relative importance of outgroup outcomes was $73.33 (95% CI [$14.98, $133.14], p = .011; see Table S2.6.3).

Mediation by impartial beneficence.

Participants' endorsement of the principle of impartial beneficence mediated 18.03% of the effect of the systematic moral reflection on participants' subsequent donations (95% CI [7.63%, 30.55%], p = .001). The indirect effect through an increase in impartial beneficence was $90.68 (95% CI [35.98, 151.52], p = .001; see Table S2.6.3). This corroborates the earlier finding that systematic moral reflection was mediated by participants learning moral lessons related to impartial beneficence.

Mediation by increased moral concern for outgroups.

The experimental group's increased moral concern for the outgroup of the second dilemma, mediated 14.13% of the effect of the reflection on subsequent donations (95% CI [6.16%, 24.22%], p = .001). The indirect effect of the reflection on money donated through an increase in moral weight assigned to the outgroup was $71.04 (95% CI [29.38, 115.67], p = .001; see Table S2.6.3).

How Did Systematic Reflection Promote Prosocial Learning?

Consistent with Prediction 2, and as in Experiment 1, systematic moral reflection aligned how positively versus negatively people felt about their first decision with how prosocial it was (τ = .140, z = 4.70, p < .001). During the systematic reflection, participants also rated their decision strategy more positively when it had produced a more prosocial decision (τ = .117, z = 3.92, p < .001). For participants who completed the systematic moral reflection, feeling bad about the first decision significantly predicted subsequent increases in the outgroup prosociality of the second decision (τ = -0.109, z = -3.84, p < .001) and so did being dissatisfied with the strategy of the first decision (τ = -0.120, z = -4.24, p < .001).

A Boundary Condition on Transfer (Experiment S2)

Experiment 2 showed that the benefits of systematic moral reflection can extend to a second dilemma involving a different outgroup. A further preregistered experiment (Experiment S2; N = 966) identified a boundary condition for the breadth of this transfer effect. Experiment S2 used the same first decision as Experiments 1 and 2, but changed the structure of the second social dilemma. In the first decision, participants traded off their own and their family's time against the needs of food-insecure people in their community, and those assigned to the experimental condition then completed the same systematic moral reflection. All participants subsequently took the role of a CEO deciding how to allocate $194,725 of unexpected profit between a Christmas bonus for the company's 100 employees (and themselves) and a donation to Helen Keller International, a charity that treats vitamin A deficiency in young children in Africa.

This second dilemma differed from the one used in Experiment 2 in several important ways.

First, unlike in Experiment 2, where the ingroup remained the same, Experiment S2's second decision replaced the protagonist's family with a new group of less well-off people outside of the protagonist's family. Second, the outgroup changed from fellow Americans to children in Africa.

The full method and results are reported in Section S4 of the Supplemental Materials.

For the pair of social dilemmas used in Experiment S2, SMR had no detectable effect on this second decision. Participants who reflected donated about the same amount (M = $70,720, SD = $51,200) as participants who did not (M = $71,150, SD = $45,570), t(940.73) = -0.14, p = .889, d = -0.01, 95% CI [-0.14, 0.12] (Figure 2D). The preregistered nonparametric ANCOVA also found no difference between the two conditions' regression curves (h = 0.09, p = .992), and an exploratory test found no evidence that the curves were not parallel (h = 0.09, p = .966). This null result does not reflect insufficient power. With 966 participants the design had 80% power to detect an effect of d = 0.18 in a two-tailed test, and the confidence interval excludes the effects observed in Experiment 1 (d = 0.43, 95% CI [0.24, 0.62]), Experiment S1 (d = 0.31, 95% CI [0.12, 0.50]) and Experiment 2 (d = 0.37, 95% CI [0.26, 0.48]).

In fact, there was no overlap between the confidence intervals of Experiments 2 and S2.

The intervention of Experiment S2 engaged participants as it did in Experiments 1 and 2.

Of those in the SMR condition, 77.6% reported having learned a useful lesson, a rate comparable to Experiments 1 and 2, and the lessons they reported were no less relevant to the second decision: 61.3% expressed an orientation toward impartial altruism and the greater good, and 67.6% were general resolutions about how to decide rather than what to do (Table S4.1). This suggests the bottleneck in transfer was not learning useful lessons but applying them to a new kind of scenario. Moreover, the learning signal itself operated as predicted (Prediction 2): the worse participants felt about their first decision, the more their second decision changed (see Section S4). We return to this in the Discussion below.

Discussion

In Experiment 2, reflecting on how they had treated one outgroup increased participants' prosociality toward a different outgroup, via greater moral concern for the outgroup, stronger endorsement of the principle of impartial beneficence, and corresponding changes in the strategies they used to make the second social dilemma decision. Once again, participants' decisions, attitudes, process-tracing data, and written lessons converged on the same conclusion: SMR taught people to consider more ways in which their decisions might affect the outgroup and to take those effects more seriously.

While Experiment 2 demonstrated a transfer effect, the absence of transfer in Experiment S2 indicates that its scope is limited. The two experiments differed only in the second decision, and in Experiment S2 that decision changed both the outgroup and the ingroup. We return to what this implies about the boundary of transfer in the General Discussion, after integrating the results of Experiment 3.

Experiment 3: Evaluation Against State-of-the-Art Approaches to Improving Intergroup

Relations on a Real Social Issue Experiments 1, S1, 2, and S2 measured outgroup prosociality via hypothetical decisions. This experiment addresses this limitation by showing that SMR can motivate people to engage in costly helping of a stigmatized social group in the real world. Concretely, we test whether SMR can increase how much real money people donate to help transgender individuals and immigrants, and how it affects the affective and cognitive components of their attitudes toward these groups. Prior research has evaluated a range of interventions for reducing prejudice against transgender people (Flores et al., 2021; Tompkins et al., 2015), including some that have backfired among members of dominant groups (Flores et al., 2021). This motivates both developing more effective interventions and evaluating them more thoroughly-the two goals Experiment 3 pursues.

SMR is designed to foster moral learning, which can produce lasting moral change.

Experiments 1, S1, 2, and S2 measured only the intervention's immediate effects, leaving it unclear whether SMR's effects were fleeting or lasting. Experiment 3 tests whether they last for at least one week and, in a second follow-up, whether they are still detectable about four and a half months later.

A further question is whether SMR, which takes more time and effort, has any benefits over perspective taking and empathy induction, two approaches that are thoroughly established in the social psychology literature (e.g., Batson, Polycarpou, et al., 1997; Galinsky & Moskowitz, 2000). While those interventions promote compassion toward a specific outgroup (e.g., Batson, Polycarpou, et al., 1997), SMR teaches people to weigh the impact of their decisions on any distant stranger more heavily. By increasing the weight assigned to others' welfare in general rather than inducing feeling toward one group, SMR might make people more prosocial toward groups that were never the focus of reflection. This differentiates SMR from empathy induction through perspective-taking, which has been found to motivate people to help one person or group at the expense of the greater good (e.g., Batson, Batson, et al., 1995; Batson, Klein, et al., 1995). We therefore predict that SMR will be uniquely effective at improving the effectiveness of people's donations, more likely to transfer to decisions about helping other outgroups, and less likely to make people prefer ineffective help for transgender individuals over highly effective charities helping other groups.

Methods

To evaluate SMR against the two state-of-the-art interventions for reducing prejudice against an outgroup and a neutral control group, we conducted a preregistered multi-arm pretest-posttest randomized controlled trial. We measured the effectiveness of each intervention both immediately after its delivery and in two follow-up assessments, at least 7 days and about four and a half months later. We preregistered the experimental procedure and data analysis at https://aspredicted.org/zn7ie7.pdf for the immediate effects and at https://aspredicted.org/zp8w7d.pdf for the follow-up effects. The second follow-up (Part 3) was designed and pre-registered after Part 2 (https://aspredicted.org/2pz5ei.pdf).

In addition to asking people to deliberate, SMR also guides them to take the perspective of the outgroup members they have helped or harmed and to empathize with them. Previous work has shown that perspective taking and empathy induction are sufficient to promote outgroup prosociality on their own (e.g., Adida et al., 2018; Batson, Sager, et al., 1997; Clore & Jeffery, 1972; Dovidio et al., 2004; Galinsky & Moskowitz, 2000; Johnson, 1967; Rodríguez Chatruc & Rozo, 2024; Simonovits et al., 2018). These interventions have been found to be effective across a wide range of outgroups (Batson, Polycarpou, et al., 1997; Todd & Galinsky, 2014), including transgender people (Flores et al., 2021; Tompkins et al., 2015) and immigrants (Adida et al., 2018; Rodríguez Chatruc & Rozo, 2024).

Participants

We aimed to recruit 2,400 adult participants on Prolific (600 per condition). For ethical and scientific reasons, we used Prolific's screening function to restrict participation to adult US citizens residing in the US who are fluent in English, do not identify as LGBTQ+, and have an approval rate of at least 95% on Prolific.

Our rationale for the target sample size was as follows. Anticipating that effects diminish over time, we aimed for 80% power to detect effects of d = 0.14 or larger at α = .05 in one-sided tests, adjusting for each outcome's pre-intervention value. Using pilot data, we estimated that the correlation between pre-and post-intervention scores would be at least 0.61. With these inputs, ChatGPT (OpenAI, 2025-2026) calculated that we would need about 400 analyzable participants per condition. We recruited 600 per condition to allow for data-quality exclusions and for attrition at the one-week follow-up.

Due to a technical error, the systematic moral reflection (SMR) materials did not display correctly to the 69 participants who entered that condition during the first two days of data collection; we detected and corrected the error on the second collection day. Because these participants did not receive the intended manipulation, we excluded their data from all analyses; we compensated them in full, and we recruited replacement participants.

Part 1. In total, 2,493 participants entered the Part 1 survey after the error was corrected. Following the preregistered criteria, 62 participants who identified as transgender or as unsure/questioning were screened out, and seven ended the survey before random assignment. Of the 2,424 randomized participants who completed the survey, 114 were excluded for failing more than one of three attention and comprehension checks, completing the survey in less than one-third of their condition's median completion time, or not being confidently classified as unassisted human respondents by Prolific's authenticity checks;

Section S1.6 of the Supplemental Materials details the checks, pass rates, and exclusion cascade. Participants who endorsed false descriptions of the charities' aims (pass rate 67.9%) were additionally excluded from all donation analyses. The final Part 1 analytic sample comprised 2,310 participants (582 SMR, 574 neutral reflection, 596 empathy induction, 558 perspective taking), of whom 1,217 were women and 1,093 were men (mean age 37.2 years, SD = 12.5, range 18-106). Based on Prolific's demographic records, 65.1% of participants were White, 16.9% Black, 7.4% of mixed ethnicity, 6.3% Asian, and 3.1% from other ethnicities (1.2% unavailable or undisclosed); all resided in the United States, 91.9% were born there, and 45.1% were employed full-time. The final longitudinal sample comprised 2,007 participants who met the preregistered criteria in both parts (EI: n = 521; SMR: n = 504; NR: n = 497; PT: n = 485). Missing follow-up data were not imputed. Retention exceeded the target of 400 analyzable participants per condition, and the observed correlation between pre-intervention and follow-up donations was r = .70, higher than the value assumed when planning the sample size. As a result, the realized power exceeded the planned level. We preregistered that Part 1 data collection would continue until 2400 participants had completed the first survey or until 14 days after launch, whichever came first. We invited each participant who completed Part 1 to Part 2 seven days later. As preregistered, Part 2 data collection closed 7 days after the last Part 1 participants were invited to Part 2.

After providing informed consent, participants were asked whether they identified as transgender and whether they identified as an immigrant in the USA. We then measured the baseline values of several key dependent variables: positive/negative feelings toward transgender people and immigrants, welfare-tradeoff ratios, and donation-based measures of i) outgroup prosociality, ii) outgroup hostility, and iii) effectiveness focus. Participants then received their condition-specific intervention.

We measured empathic concern for transgender people immediately after each intervention (empathy induction, perspective taking, neutral reflection) or after the part of the intervention designed to induce empathic concern (SMR). Next, participants completed the postintervention measures. The first set of post-intervention measures was the two donation-based measures with an equivalent set of different charities. Participants then rated their feelings toward transgender people and immigrants again (feeling thermometers; Tyler & Iyengar, 2024).

Next, they were presented with a Likert scale measuring their support for pro-and anti-transgender policies (policy support). After that, participants indicated how (un)willing they would be to engage in different types of social relationships with transgender people and immigrants (stigma). The following scales measured participants' post-intervention welfaretradeoff ratios and moral concern for the two outgroups. The survey's final measures were selfother overlap with members of the two outgroups, impartial beneficence, and blatant dehumanization (see Measures for details).

Each participant who completed their first survey was invited to the follow-up survey 7 days later. The follow-up survey included the feeling thermometers from Part 1, welfare tradeoff ratio measures for transgender people and immigrants, new sets of donation decisions measuring outgroup prosociality, outgroup hostility, and effectiveness focus, along with measures of their support for policies protecting transgender people, stigma against transgender people and immigrants, moral concern for transgender people, perceived self-other overlap with transgender people and immigrants, blatant dehumanization of transgender people and immigrants, and impartial beneficence.

The second follow-up survey (Part 3) was titled "Decisions about Money and Time, Part 3" and did not mention transgender people, immigrants, or the earlier intervention. It remained open for 14 days, with one reminder, and paid $5.00 (median completion time: about 15 minutes). To minimize reactivity, behavior was measured before attitudes. Participants first made three incentive-compatible donation decisions. Each time, they decided how much money to donate to a charity ($0-$100) if they won the experiment's $100 raffle. The three charities supported transgender people, victims of natural disasters, or the arts, respectively. Participants then made three allocation decisions in which they split money between a charity helping transgender people and another charity focussed on three other causes (arts and culture, disaster victims, and the participant's local community). None of the charities in Part 3 had been used in Part 1 or Part 2. Participants then completed the welfare tradeoff ratio measures, the blatant dehumanization scale, the feeling thermometers, and the willingness-to-interact scale from Parts 1 and 2. Next, they could take two real prosocial actions: adding their name to a collective letter of support for transgender people and writing a supportive note for delivery to a transgender person through a nonprofit letter-writing program. Finally, they placed transgender people, LGB people, immigrants, and members of their immediate family in their moral circle separately for four types of moral concern, beginning with the obligation to help them (positive obligations), and completed a funnel debriefing about the purpose of the study. Because the Prolific study initially linked to an outdated draft of the survey, 80 participants (6%) first completed a version that did not record the welfare tradeoff ratios; Section S1.7.3 of the Supplemental Materials describes how we handled this, and Section S1.7 reports the full methods of Part 3.

Materials

The full text of the social dilemma scenario used in this experiment is provided in Section S5.4 of the Supplemental Materials.

Systematic Moral Reflection Prompts

The systematic moral reflection prompts were identical to those used in Experiment 2, except that the prompts guiding participants to empathize with the people who were most significantly affected by their decision were reworded. The rewording helped participants take the perspective of those helped or harmed by their decision and then step out of it afterward to reduce empathic distress. These changes were informed by prior research on the complementary effects of imagining how another person feels versus how oneself would feel in their situation (Batson, Early, et al., 1997). Table 3 describes the revised perspective-taking prompts in detail.

Table 3 Prompts Guiding Participants to Assess the Outcomes for the Person Who Was Harmed the Most as Used in the SMR Condition of Experiment 3 1 Now picture this person or one of these people (NAME).

Please describe the negative impact(s) they experienced (e.g., how they were harmed), the situation they were in when it happened, and how it is affecting them now.

Try to describe this in terms of their life and circumstances (e.g., "When Kyle arrived at the food bank at noon, it was closed. A sign said that today's opening hours had been shortened to 8-11 a.m. because staff are ill and volunteers cancelled. It took him two hours to walk there. He hasn't eaten in two days. He lost his job two months ago, and the food bank was his last hope for feeding his family this weekend."). 2

Now imagine that you are in the situation of this individual and that the same bad things are happening to you. We will ask you a few questions as if you were in that situation. This is just to help you understand what this kind of situation would be like from the inside. (This prompt and the following question still refer to NAME.). 3

How does the negative impact of the event you described above relate to your wants and needs? (Please answer as if you were in this situation yourself; for instance, "The food bank being closed meant that I had to survive on tap water and salt for the third day in a row. It meant being denied even the most basic thing I need to survive. It also meant being unable to feed my family for three days in a row, watching them starve, and feeling like a complete failure.") 4

What thoughts are going through your mind as this bad thing is happening to you? (Please answer as if you were in this situation yourself.) 5

How does this bad event make you feel emotionally? (Please answer as if you were in this situation yourself.) 6

Now gently let go of imagining yourself in that situation and return your attention to your actual life in the present moment.

From this safe vantage point, we'll focus again on the person in the scenario (NAME). Note. The prompts for guiding people to assess their action's effect on those who benefited the most were equivalent. The word NAME is a placeholder for the name the participant used to describe the person who was most negatively affected by their decision.

Neutral Reflection Prompts

In Experiments 1, S1, 2, and S2, the control condition was chosen to assess the degree to which SMR boosts what people learn from the outcome of a social dilemma decision compared with what they would learn without any support. As a result, the SMR condition was longer than the control condition and included several elements the control condition did not.

The present experiment addresses these limitations with a time-matched neutral reflection condition that controls for duration, difficulty, and deliberation. Controlling for time and difficulty eliminates potential confounds due to self-selection, drop-out, or exhaustion. Controlling for reflection helps distinguish the unique effects of systematic moral reflection from merely shifting people from intuitive to deliberative processing (Rand & Epstein, 2014).

The neutral reflection prompts asked participants to reflect on enjoyable everyday activities. The prompts were designed to match the amount of reflection, time, and writing required by the SMR intervention without evoking reflection on either morally relevant outcomes or decision-making processes.

Participants first listed four everyday activities they find enjoyable and selected two of them to reflect on. For each of the two activities, participants answered seventeen questions about the activity, including where, when, and how they engage in it, how they experience it, how it fits into their life, and the last time they engaged in it. Afterward, participants answered seven questions about how the two activities are similar, how they are different, and how they complement each other.

Perspective-Taking Intervention

Following Galinsky and Moskowitz (2000), the perspective-taking intervention was framed as testing people's ability to construct life event details from visual information.

Participants were shown an image of a transgender woman with pink hair in a thinking posture sitting on a couch in front of a designer table with a laptop, pencils, paper, and a cellphone. 3Participants were then asked to write a brief passage describing a typical day in the life of the individual in the photograph from a first-person perspective. They were instructed to try to take the perspective of the photographed individual by going through a typical day in their shoes, as if they were that person. Participants were prompted to write about the woman's goals and intentions as well as her thoughts and feelings. They were required to write at least 500 characters (about 100 words) before they could proceed to the next page. On the next page, participants rated to what extent they had imagined the woman's thoughts and feelings, to what extent they had been objective and detached while writing the essay, and to what extent they used AI to write the passage.

Manipulation check.

Of the 655 participants assigned to the perspective-taking condition, 608 (92.8%) completed the writing exercise and the subsequent self-reports; their passages averaged 148.7 words (SD = 47.2). Participants reported having imagined the woman's thoughts and feelings to a considerable degree (M = 4.12, SD = 1.43, on a scale from 0 to 6), with 70.2% giving a rating of 4 or higher and only 1.8% reporting that they had not done so at all. They reported having been objective and detached to a significantly lesser degree (M = 2.90, SD = 1.86), t(607) = 13.04, p < .001, dz = 0.53. Self-reported use of AI was rare: 94.7% reported none at all, and only 2.8% reported using it moderately or more. Participants therefore engaged with the perspective-taking instructions largely as intended, and the comparison between SMR and perspective taking is unlikely to be attributable to a failure of the perspectivetaking manipulation.

Empathy Induction Intervention

Participants watched a short video (1:17 min) in which a transgender woman is interviewed about being bullied in school 4 . Prior to watching the video, they were instructed to "try to imagine how the person being interviewed feels about what has happened and how it has affected their life." Afterward, they were asked to write a paragraph about how the transgender woman interviewed in the video must have felt when she was bullied. To enforce engagement with these prompts, participants had to spend at least 100 seconds on the page and had to enter at least 20 characters. The wording of the instructions closely followed the well- 4 The video we used is available in the project's OSF repository.

established empathy induction method by Batson, Polycarpou, et al. (1997) and its adaptation to videos by Dovidio et al. (2004).

To ensure that participants heard the sound of the video, we required them to pass a sound check before proceeding to the empathy induction intervention. The sound check required them to correctly report the order in which a low tone, a high tone, and a mid-frequency tone were played. The order of these tones was randomized. The JavaScript implementing the sound check was written with ChatGPT (OpenAI, 2025 (OpenAI, -2026) ) and was tested by the authors before data collection.

We selected this video from seven candidates in a pilot study (N = 280): relative to a neutral video, it increased empathic concern by d = 0.96 while remaining acceptable regarding distress, disgust, threat, reactance, trust, and comprehensibility (see Section S1.5 of the Supplemental Materials).

Manipulation check.

Of the 653 participants assigned to the empathy-induction condition, 624 (95.6%) completed the writing task, spending a median of 186 s on the intervention page (the video itself lasted 1 min 17 s); the median participant wrote 22 words.

Immediately afterwards, 622 (95.3%) completed the empathic-concern measure (α = .97) and reported moderate to high empathic concern (M = 4.48, SD = 1.78, on a scale from 1 to 7), with 48.6% at 5 or above. This level of empathic concern was significantly higher than in the perspective-taking condition (M = 3.96, SD = 1.77), d = 0.30, 95% CI [0.18, 0.41].

Measures

Welfare Tradeoff Ratios. A person's welfare tradeoff ratio for another person is the decision weight they assign to that person's well-being divided by the decision weight they assign to their own well-being. We measured each participant's welfare tradeoff ratios for four others from different social groups: a transgender individual, an immigrant, a person who is lesbian, gay, or bisexual, and someone from their immediate family. We measured these four welfare tradeoff ratios using an adapted version of the lambda slider measure developed by Qi et al. (2025). For each of the four people, participants were asked to imagine they could increase their own well-being and the well-being of that other person. They then used the lambda slider to select the added amounts of well-being for themselves and the other entity that looked best to them. The lambda slider is constructed such that the slider position generating the combination that is best according to a participant's social preferences is equal to their welfare tradeoff ratio.

Donation-based measures of outgroup prosociality and outgroup hostility.

We created three blocks of four incentive-compatible donation decisions each. Each participant completed the first two blocks in random order: one before the intervention and one after. We presented the third block in the follow-up survey.

Participants knew that, for each part, we would randomly select two participants and pay out one of their decisions. In each donation decision, participants decided how to split $100 between themselves and a real charity. Each trial involved a different charity. No charity was presented twice to the same participant. Each block included four types of charities: one helping transgender people (e.g., Trans Lifeline), one helping immigrants (e.g., National Immigration Law Center), one seeking to restrict transgender rights (e.g., Women's Liberation Front), and one aiming to restrict immigration and/or reduce protections for immigrants (e.g., Institute for Sound Public Policy). We used the amount of money a participant donated to the protransgender and pro-immigration charities as a measure of their outgroup prosociality toward transgender people and immigrants, respectively. Conversely, we used the amount of money a participant donated to the anti-trans and anti-immigration charities as a measure of their hostility toward transgender people and immigrants, respectively. We informed participants of each charity's name, primary beneficiaries, programs, impact, and Charity Navigator ratings. We also provided participants with a link to each charity's website and its Charity Navigator profile. We drafted the donation-option descriptions with Claude Opus (Anthropic, 2025 (Anthropic, -2026) from publicly available information about each charity and reviewed, verified, and edited them before use.

Donation-based measures of effectiveness focus. We measured how effectively people pursue their prosocial motives by how they split a donation between a highly effective charity and a symbolic or commemorative organization serving the same population. Each allocation decision was incentive-compatible because it was included in the set of decisions that two randomly selected participants might make. We created three blocks of three allocation decisions each. In every block, participants split $100 between i) a highly cost-effective global health charity (Helen Keller International, the Malaria Consortium, or the Against Malaria In addition to these behavioral measures, we administered eight measures of participants' feelings and beliefs about the target groups. Table 4 summarizes each measure's construct, source, and response format; Section S1.4 of the Supplemental Materials provides the full descriptions.

Reliability. All multi-item scales' internal-consistency estimates (Cronbach's α) were acceptable to excellent (αs ≥ .79) at both assessments. We report the Cronbach's α values computed from our dataset in Table S1.3.

Data Analysis Preregistration.

We followed the preregistered analysis plan (https://aspredicted.org/zp8w7d.pdf) and conducted additional exploratory analyses, clearly labeled as such. In general, our analyses use linear models with covariates measuring preexisting individual differences in the dependent variable, when available, or one or more closely related variables, otherwise. The linear models also included interaction effects between this measure of pre-existing differences in the construct of interest and the experimental condition.

All covariates were grand-mean centered. As preregistered, we used one-tailed tests for all directional preregistered hypotheses and did not control for multiple comparisons.

Effect size estimates.

For each model, we additionally report a covariate-adjusted standardized effect size, dadj, computed as the model's condition coefficient divided by the raw standard deviation of the outcome among analyzed participants (Morris & DeShon, 2002). This retains the metric of Cohen's d while using the more precise covariate-adjusted estimate of the group difference; in models with a Condition × baseline interaction, dadj estimates the effect at the sample-mean baseline.

Analyses of Part 3. We followed the Part 3 preregistration (https://aspredicted.org/2pz5ei.pdf), except for the deviations listed in Table S1.7.1 of the Supplemental Materials. The primary contrast compared SMR with neutral reflection. Outcomes measured at all three post-intervention assessments (donations to charities helping transgender people, welfare tradeoff ratios, and dehumanization) were analyzed with mixed-effects models for repeated measures (R package mmrm; Sabanés Bové et al., 2025) that included condition, assessment, and their interaction; the pre-intervention measure of the outcome (for dehumanization, closely related pre-intervention attitudes) and its interactions with assessment and condition; and gender. These models used an unstructured covariance matrix and Kenward-Roger degrees of freedom (Kenward & Roger, 1997). As preregistered, these models used all available observations of the 1,156 SMR and NR participants in the Part 1 sample, including those who did not complete Part 3 (620 did); this yields unbiased estimates if the missing observations are missing at random given the observed ones. As in Parts 1 and 2, the donations of participants who failed a part's charity-comprehension check were excluded from that part's observations. Each prediction was tested with the SMR-NR contrast in Part 3, computed with the R package emmeans (Lenth, 2025), and decay was estimated as the change in this contrast from immediately after the intervention to Part 3. Outcomes measured only in Part 3 were analyzed with the linear models described above. As in Parts 1 and 2, directional predictions were tested one-tailed, and dadj was computed with the standard deviation of the outcome in Part 3. The two tests of whether the transfer effects persisted were Holm-corrected (Holm, 1979). Ascent of Man scale (Kteily et al., 2015) Slider from 0 to 100 rating "how evolved" each group is perceived to be were significantly improved (see Figure 3). Below, we report these effects from strongest to weakest.

Behavior Toward the Target Group Donations to charities helping the target group. Consistent with Prediction 1, the component of people's attitudes toward the target group that SMR improved the most was their behavior toward the outgroup, as demonstrated by an increase in the costly prosocial behavior of donating money to charities helping the outgroup. As predicted, participants who completed SMR subsequently allocated more money to a charity helping transgender people than participants who reflected on leisure activities. After adjusting for pre-reflection donations and gender, SMR participants donated $9.93 more (out of $100) than neutral-reflection participants, b = 9.93, t(843) = 6.09, p < .001 (one-tailed). Compared to neutral reflection, SMR increased the average raw donation amount from $36.52 (SD = 33.53) to $46.10 (SD = 38.12), dadj = 0.27, 95% CI [0.19, 0.36], and the median donation increased from $30 to $50, which meant splitting the endowment 50/50 (see Figure 4). The effect did not depend on participants' pre-reflection giving (Condition × baseline interaction, p = .921), indicating that SMR raised donations across the full range of baseline generosity toward the target group, rather than only among those already inclined to support them.

Willingness to interact with the target group. SMR also increased participants' willingness to choose a transgender person as a social partner. Adjusting for gender as well as transgender-related warmth, donations, and welfare tradeoff ratios at pre-test, participants who completed SMR reported significantly greater willingness to interact with transgender individuals than participants who reflected on leisure activities, b = 0.17, t(1092) = 2.74, p = .003 (onetailed), dadj = 0.11, 95% CI [0.03, 0.18]. The effect did not depend on participants' pre-reflection warmth toward transgender people (Condition × baseline interaction, p = .151).

Effect on the WTR for the target group.

We next asked whether SMR shifted participants' welfare tradeoff ratios (WTRs) toward transgender people, that is, the weight they placed on a transgender stranger's welfare relative to their own. Regressing post-reflection WTR on condition while controlling for pre-reflection WTR and gender, participants in the SMR condition placed significantly greater weight on transgender welfare than those in the neutralreflection condition, b = 0.08, t(1056) = 3.21, p < .001 (one-tailed); the raw mean WTR rose from 0.33 following neutral reflection to 0.45 following SMR. The standardized effect was small but reliably positive, dadj = 0.12, 95% CI [0.05, 0.19]. This increase was qualified by a significant Condition × baseline-WTR interaction, b = -0.10, t(1056) = -2.38, p = .018: the SMR-induced gain was larger among participants who began with lower welfare weights on transgender people and smaller among those who already placed high weight on their welfare.

Cognitions About the Outgroup

SMR reduced the dehumanization of transgender people. As predicted, SMR significantly reduced blatant dehumanization of transgender people. Adjusting for gender and baseline attitudes (pre-reflection warmth, donations, and welfare tradeoff ratios), participants who completed SMR dehumanized transgender people significantly less than participants who completed neutral reflection, b = -5.06, t(1091) = -3.57, p < .001 (one-tailed), dadj = -0.18, 95% CI [-0.28, -0.08]. This effect did not depend on participants' baseline attitudes towards transgender people (Condition × baseline interaction, p = .417).

Policy beliefs.

SMR also increased support for policies protecting or expanding the rights of transgender people. Adjusting for pre-reflection welfare tradeoff ratios, warmth, and gender, participants in the SMR condition expressed significantly greater support for policies protecting or extending transgender rights than participants in the neutral-reflection condition, b = 0.16, t(1093) = 2.35, p = .010 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.10, 95% CI [0.02, 0.18]. The effect did not vary with baseline welfare tradeoff ratios (p = .190).

No effect on the perceived moral standing of transgender people. Contrary to our prediction, SMR did not move transgender people closer to the center of participants' moral circles. Controlling for the same baseline covariates, the effect of condition on moral-circle placement was nonsignificant and directionally negative, b = -0.004, t(1052) = -0.36, p = .639 (one-tailed; p = .721, two-tailed), dadj = -0.02, 95% CI [-0.11, 0.08].

Feelings Toward the

Target Group SMR also improved the affective component of participants' attitudes toward the target group. Controlling for pre-reflection warmth and gender, participants who completed SMR reported significantly warmer feelings toward transgender people than participants who reflected on leisure activities, b = 4.03, t(1151) = 5.49, p < .001 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.13, 95% CI [0.08, 0.17]. A significant Condition × baseline-warmth interaction, b = -0.08, t(1151) = -3.49, p < .001, indicated that SMR produced the largest increases in warmth among participants with the least favorable initial feelings toward transgender people. How Do the Effects of SMR Compare to Empathy Induction and Perspective-Taking? We next asked whether the benefits of SMR merely reflect demand characteristics, perspective-taking, or empathy, or whether SMR outperforms established prejudice-reduction interventions that elicit only those processes but not systematic moral reflection. To this end, we compare the SMR condition against two state-of-the-art prejudice-reduction interventions based on empathy induction (EI) and perspective-taking (PT), using the same covariate-adjusted models with SMR as the reference group and one-tailed tests of the preregistered prediction that SMR would outperform each comparison condition.

SMR Increases Costly Helping More Than Standard Prejudice Reduction Interventions

Donations to charities helping the target group. Consistent with Prediction 6, SMR outperformed every comparison condition on motivating people to help the target group at a personal cost, as measured by donations to a charity helping transgender people (see Figure 4). Adjusting for gender and pre-reflection donations, the SMR group donated $3.49 more than the empathy-induction group, t(1617) = 2.27, p = .012 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.10, 95% CI [0.01, 0.18] and $5.63 more than the perspective-taking group, t(1617) = 3.64, p < .001 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.16, 95% CI [0.07, 0.24]. Because empathy induction and perspective-taking plausibly produce at least as much experimental demand and emotional engagement as SMR, SMR's advantage over these active interventions cannot readily be attributed to demand characteristics or transient emotional arousal.

Moreover, because the empathy-induction and perspective-taking conditions each deliver one of SMR's familiar ingredients on its own, SMR's advantage over both interventions indicates that its benefits are not reducible to the empathy or perspective-taking components it contains.

Therefore, its distinctive element of guiding people to learn lessons from their own past moral decisions may contribute beyond these established components. Moreover, SMR's significant benefit over neutral reflection shows that its benefit is not merely due to putting people into a more reflective and analytical mindset either.

An exploratory follow-up analysis addressed the further alternative explanation that SMR outperformed the comparison interventions simply because its higher intensity induced more empathic concern. It did not. Participants in the SMR condition reported significantly less empathic concern for transgender people than participants in the empathy-induction condition, b = -0.48, SE = 0.11, t(2166) = -4.42, p < .001, and the neutral-reflection group, b = -0.36, SE = 0.11, t(2166) = -3.34, p = .001, while donating more than both.foot_3 SMR thus increased costly helping without increasing the affective response that empathy induction is designed to elicit.

Table S2.9.4 reports the corresponding contrasts for all candidate mediators. Note. Distributions of donations to charities helping transgender people immediately after the intervention (in $ of the $100 endowment) in the systematic-moral-reflection (SMR), empathyinduction (EI), perspective-taking (PT), and neutral-reflection (NR) conditions. For each condition, the box plot summarizes the raw donations (horizontal line = median; box = interquartile range; whiskers = 1.5 × the interquartile range), the gray dashes to its left show the individual participants' donations, and the shaded curve to its right shows their smoothed distribution. The black horizontal line to the right of each distribution marks the covariateadjusted mean donation in that condition (adjusting for participants' pre-intervention donation and gender), and the green error bar around it is the 95% confidence interval of that adjusted mean. Higher values indicate that more of the $100 was donated to the charity helping the outgroup.

Other Behaviors

Welfare tradeoff ratios for the target group. SMR increased participants' decision weights for the target group's welfare more than perspective-taking, b = 0.06, t(2105) = 2.36, p = .009 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.09, 95% CI [0.01, 0.16], while its advantage over empathy induction was marginal, b = 0.04, t(2105) = 1.62, p = .053 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.06, 95% CI [-0.01, 0.13].

Willingness to interact with the target group. SMR increased participants' willingness to choose a transgender person as a social partner relative to perspective-taking, b = 0.14, t(2176) = 2.36, p = .009 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.09, 95% CI [0.02, 0.17], but not relative to empathy induction, b = 0.03, p = .292 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.02, 95% CI [-0.054, 0.096].

Cognitions

Policy beliefs. SMR increased support for transgender-protective policies relative to perspective-taking, b = 0.16, t(2178) = 2.38, p = .009 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.10, 95% CI [0.02, 0.18], but not relative to empathy induction, b = 0.07, p = .139 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.04, 95% CI [-0.04, 0.12].

Beliefs about the moral standing of transgender people. SMR moved transgender people closer to the center of participants' moral circles relative to perspective-taking, b = 0.02, t(2103) = 2.09, p = .018 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.10, 95% CI [0.01, 0.19], but not relative to empathy induction, b = 0.01, p = .285 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.03, 95% CI [-0.06, 0.12].

Dehumanization.

According to an exploratory analysis, SMR reduced dehumanization of transgender people more than the perspective-taking intervention, b = 3.88, t(2176) = 2.68, p = .007, dadj = 0.14, 95% CI [0.04, 0.24], but not relative to empathy induction, b = 1.18, t(2176) = 0.83, p = .407, dadj = 0.04, 95% CI [-0.06, 0.14], although the difference favored SMR numerically.

Feelings

Warmth toward the target group. SMR made participants feel warmer toward transgender people than either of the two established interventions. Thus, relative to SMR, warmth toward transgender people was significantly lower in the perspective-taking condition, b = -2.70, t(2301) = -3.84, p < .001 (one-tailed), dadj = -0.09, 95% CI [-0.13, -0.04], and in the empathy induction condition, b = -1.29, t(2301) = -1.86, p = .031 (one-tailed), dadj = -0.04, 95% CI [-0.09, 0.00].

Transfer to Untargeted Outgroups

A key question for any prejudice-reduction intervention is whether its benefits are confined to the group featured in the intervention or transfer to other outgroups (Prediction 5).

The SMR materials concerned a decision about transgender people; neither immigrants nor lesbian, gay, and bisexual (LGB) people were mentioned in any part of this intervention. We therefore tested whether SMR nevertheless improved attitudes toward these untargeted groups relative to neutral reflection (see Figure 3). Finally, we also tested how the transgenderfocussed version of the SMR intervention affected people's attitudes toward charities seeking to protect cisgender women from transgender women.

Transfer to Attitudes Toward Immigrants

The effect of systematic moral reflection on a decision about helping transgender people transferred to improvements in all three aspects of participants' attitudes toward immigrants: affect, cognitions, and behavior (see Figure 3). The analyses reported in this section tested this hypothesis with linear models controlling for each DV's level prior to the intervention and participants' immigrant identity.

Transfer to Behavior Toward Immigrants

Donations to pro-immigrant charities. Following SMR participants donated significantly more money to charities helping immigrants and refugees (M = $49.99, SD = $35.59) than after NR (M = $43.89, SD = $33.72), b = 3.44, t(840) = 2.22, p = .013 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.10, 95% CI [0.01, 0.19]. This effect was strongest for participants who had donated less to pro-immigrant charities prior to the intervention, b = -0.11, t(840) = -2.25, p = .025.

Importance of immigrants' welfare.

Following SMR, participants assigned more weigh to immigrants' welfare (M = 0.57) than following NR (M = 0.46), b = 0.06, t(1055) = 2.94, p = .002 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.11, 95% CI [0.04, 0.18].

Willingness to partner with immigrants. Following SMR, participants reported a greater willingness to form social relationships with immigrants (M = 5.93) than after NR (M = 5.78), b = 0.15, t(1088) = 2.53, p = .006 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.12, 95% CI [0.03, 0.21].

Transfer to Beliefs About Immigrants

Dehumanization of immigrants.

According to an exploratory analysis paralleling the finding for transgender people, SMR significantly reduced the blatant dehumanization of immigrants (M = 9.16, SD = 17.26, vs. M = 11.98, SD = 19.77, after neutral reflection), b = -2.80, t(1100) = -2.70, p = .007 (two-tailed), dadj = -0.15, 95% CI [-0.26, -0.04], compared to neutral reflection. Dehumanization of the ingroup ("real Americans") was unaffected (see Section S2.4).

Moral standing of immigrants.

As with the target group, SMR did not affect where participants placed immigrants in their moral circle (M = 0.56, SD = 0.20, vs. M = 0.56, SD = 0.22, after neutral reflection), b = -0.01, t(1048) = -0.46, p = .677 (one-tailed), dadj = -0.03, 95% CI [-0.14, 0.09], compared to NR.

Transfer to Affect Toward Immigrants

Warmth toward immigrants.

A further exploratory analysis found that SMR increased participants' warmth toward immigrants (M = 72.93), b = 2.20, t(1139) = 3.89, p < .001 (twotailed), dadj = 0.09, 95% CI [0.05, 0.14], compared to NR (M = 71.80). As for warmth toward transgender people, this effect was strongest for participants with initially colder feelings toward immigrants, b = -0.07, p = .007.

Transfer to Attitudes Toward LGB People

As predicted, the effect of SMR also transferred to people's regard for people identifying as lesbian, gay, or bisexual (LGB). Compared to NR, SMR significantly increased the weight participants placed on the welfare of an LGB stranger, b = 0.09, t(1077) = 3.79, p < .001 (onetailed), dadj = 0.13, 95% CI [0.07, 0.20]. This effect was larger for participants for whom this moral weight was lower prior to the intervention (Condition × baseline interaction, b = -0.09, p = .016). As for the target group, SMR did not significantly change the moral-circle placement of

LGB people, b = -0.02, t(1062) = -1.67, p = .952 (one-tailed; p = .095, two-tailed), dadj = -0.09, 95% CI [-0.20, 0.02].

Transfer Effects on Donations to Charities Seeking to Protect People

From the Target Group

We had preregistered the prediction that SMR would reduce donations to charities campaigning for laws that restrict transgender women's rights in the name of protecting cisgender women (e.g., the Women's Liberation Front). This prediction was not supported; the effect ran in the opposite direction. Controlling for baseline donations and gender, participants who completed SMR donated significantly more to these charities than participants who reflected on leisure activities, b = 4.73, t(843) = 2.87, p = .004 (two-tailed); the test in the hypothesized direction was, accordingly, far from significant (p = .998, one-tailed). Raw mean donations rose from $25.81 (SD = 31.92) after neutral reflection to $33.04 (SD = 36.91) after SMR, a small effect, dadj = 0.14, 95% CI [0.04, 0.23]. As with donations to charities helping transgender people, the effect did not depend on participants' pre-reflection giving (Condition × baseline interaction, p = .825), indicating a fairly uniform increase across the range of baseline attitudes toward transgender people. This unexpected finding appears to contradict our findings that SMR increased support for laws protecting transgender people's rights, donations to charities helping transgender people, and the weight participants assigned to the welfare of transgender people. Crucially, because this analysis was restricted to participants who correctly identified the charities' aims (i.e., who endorsed no false description of what the four charities did), the increase in anti-transgender giving cannot be attributed to participants failing to notice that some charities sought to restrict rather than support transgender rights. This leaves the more substantive possibility that SMR broadened participants' concern for the women these charities claim to protect, or that reflection heightened engagement and giving across both sides of a contested moral issue rather than selectively favoring the target group. Consistent with these interpretations, the within-participant increase in donations to anti-transgender charities from before to after the SMR intervention was positively correlated with the corresponding increase in donations to pro-transgender charities, r(441) = .10, p = .036.

Echoing the unexpected increase relative to neutral reflection, exploratory two-tailed tests indicated that SMR participants donated more to these charities than participants in the perspective-taking condition, b = 5.25, t(1617) = 3.05, p = .002, dadj = 0.15, 95% CI [0.05, 0.25].

These donations did not differ significantly between the SMR and EI conditions (p = .606).

Three further exploratory analyses (two-tailed) characterize this unexpected effect. First, SMR increased the total amount participants donated across the pro-and anti-transgender charities, from $62.30 after neutral reflection to $79.10 (out of $200), b = 14.67, t(843) = 6.09, p < .001, dadj = 0.32, 95% CI [0.21, 0.42]; participants who completed SMR kept less for themselves. Second, over and above this general increase in giving, SMR shifted the balance of donations toward the charities supporting transgender people: controlling for its baseline value and gender, the difference between pro-and anti-transgender donations increased significantly, b = 5.29, t(843) = 2.38, p = .017, dadj = 0.12, 95% CI [0.02, 0.21]. Third, the increase in antitransgender giving was not concentrated among participants with negative attitudes toward transgender people: its interaction with baseline warmth was nonsignificant (p = .357), and the increase was descriptively larger among participants with warmer baseline attitudes (b = 5.03, p = .029, at the 75th percentile of baseline warmth vs. b = 2.71, p = .175, at the 25th). In summary, SMR increased engaged giving on both sides of a contested moral issue while still tilting the net flow of donations toward the target group. A directionally similar but nonsignificant pattern emerged for donations to charities seeking to restrict immigration, b = 2.64, p = .159. We consider possible explanations for this pattern in the Discussion.

established prejudice-reduction interventions produced this wider transfer. Whether the effect also transferred toward women is less clear.

Mechanisms: How Did SMR Improve People's Behavior Toward the Target Group?

Consistent with Prediction 2, an exploratory analysis found that the worse participants in the SMR condition felt about the decision they had reflected on, the more their donations to charities helping transgender people increased from before to after the intervention, Kendall's τ = -.12, z = -3.46, p < .001; the same held for their dissatisfaction with the strategy behind that decision, τ = -.21, z = -5.78, p < .001 (n = 443). This pattern is consistent with metacognitive moral learning.

Does SMR Operate Through a Route Distinct From Changes in Feelings and Beliefs About the Outgroup?

Prior research identified increased perceptions of oneness with the outgroup as the key mechanism through which perspective-taking reduces prejudice against outgroups (Galinsky & Moskowitz, 2000). SMR did increase perceived overlap with transgender people relative to neutral reflection, from M = 30.47 to M = 33.76 on a 0-100 scale, b = 3.28, SE = 1.65, t(2305) = 1.99, p = .046. The resulting level of self-other overlap did not differ significantly from those produced by empathy-induction, b = 2.95, t(2305) = 1.80, p = .071, or perspective-taking conditions, b = 2.70, t(2305) = 1.63, p = .104, neither of which increased perceived overlap relative to neutral reflection. To show that SMR also improves other aspects of people's moral judgment and decision-making, we preregistered testing whether SMR's effects on people's attitudes toward the outgroup persist when the linear model adjusts for participants' perceived overlap with the target group after the intervention. These analyses revealed that while perceived self-other overlap independently predicted every outcome (e.g., pro-transgender donations: b = 0.13, p < .001), it could not account for the full effects of SMR. Even after controlling for self-other-overlap, SMR still significantly increased donations to charities helping transgender people, b = 9.66, t(842) = 5.96, p < .001 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.27, 95% CI [0.18, 0.35], warmth, b = 3.53, t(1150) = 4.89, p < .001 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.11, 95% CI [0.07, 0.16], welfare tradeoff ratios, b = 0.08, t(1055) = 2.90, p = .002 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.11, 95% CI [0.04, 0.18], willingness to interact, b = 0.12, t(1091) = 2.06, p = .020 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.08, 95% CI [0.004, 0.15], and policy support, b = 0.11, t(1092) = 1.67, p = .048 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.07, 95% CI [-0.01, 0.15]. 6 Thus, the mechanism by which SMR produced attitude change cannot be reduced to increased perceived self-other overlap.

Moreover, according to an exploratory causal mediation analysis (10,000 nonparametric bootstrap samples, N = 848), the indirect effect of SMR on donations to pro-transgender charities through perceived self-other overlap was not statistically significant, ACME = 1.01, 95% CI [-0.76, 2.83], p = .279, proportion mediated = 10.5%, 95% CI [-10.6%, 29.5%], whereas the direct effect of SMR remained significant, ADE = 8.57, 95% CI [4.04, 13.11], p < .001 (total effect = 9.58, 95% CI [4.75, 14.45]). Thus, we found no reliable evidence that perceived self-other overlap mediated the effect of SMR on costly helping. This is consistent with the covariate-adjusted analyses, which showed that the effect is essentially unchanged when perceived self-other overlap is controlled.

Exploratory follow-up analyses explored whether the effect of SMR on costly helping behavior also survives controlling for other affective and belief-based mechanisms. We found that adjusting the effect of SMR on donations for participants' post-intervention empathic concern, warmth, blatant dehumanization, policy support, moral circle placement, self-other overlap, and endorsement of impartial beneficence left it essentially unchanged, with the coefficient for SMR ranging from b = 9.66 to b = 10.91 across different sets of covariates and remaining highly significant in every one (all ps < .001; see Table S2.9.1). These analyses were not preregistered, although the prediction they test follows from the account set out in the introduction.

These adjustments share a limitation: apart from empathic concern, we measured all other covariates after the donation decision they are used to explain. We therefore repeated the test with the follow-up donations as the outcome, so that every covariate preceded the outcome by one week.
