To keep these analyses comparable to the preregistered longitudinal results reported below, they use the preregistered model and exclusion criteria for the follow-up donations; the unadjusted effect is therefore the preregistered one, b = 2.80, SE = 1.58, t(784) = 1.77, p = .038 (one-tailed). Adjusting for all seven post-intervention measures of feelings and beliefs simultaneously did not diminish this effect but increased it, b = 3.92, SE = 1.56, t(672) = 2.51, p = .012, compared with b = 3.41 for the unadjusted model re-estimated on the same complete cases. Only two covariates attenuated the effect appreciably: the welfare tradeoff ratio, which reduced it by 27%, from b = 3.18 to b = 2.33, SE = 1.56, t(755) = 1.49, p = .136, and perceived self-other overlap, which reduced it by 12%, to b = 2.46, SE = 1.56, t(783) = 1.58, p = .116. We return to perceived self-other overlap when we report the preregistered test of the same question at follow-up. Unless otherwise noted, the exploratory tests reported here are two-tailed (see Table S2.9.2).

A simultaneous multiple-mediator model of the follow-up donations told the same story (1,000 bootstrap resamples, n = 662). The direct effect of SMR remained substantial, 4.07, 95% CI [1.11, 6.97], and statistically significant, p = .010. Among the eight candidate mediators, only the welfare tradeoff ratio showed a positive indirect effect with an interval that excluded zero (0.41, 95% CI [0.02, 0.88]). Together, the seven measures of feelings and beliefs about the outgroup accounted for none of the effect, -0.70, 95% CI [-2.03, 0.60], and the indirect effect through perceived self-other overlap was indistinguishable from zero, 0.01, 95% CI [-0.21, 0.24]. The contrast between the two sets of pathways pointed in the predicted direction but was not statistically significant, 1.11, 95% CI [-0.22, 2.42] (see Table S2.9.3). Regardless thereof, the results show that SMR's effect on costly helping cannot be reduced to changes in how participants felt or what they believed about the outgroup. However, SMR's effect could be mediated by the weight people give to others' welfare.

Are the Effects Mediated by Increased Decision Weights for the

Welfare of Transgender People?

We next tested the preregistered hypothesis, and the account's fourth prediction, that SMR increased donations to charities helping transgender people by increasing the weight participants placed on transgender people's welfare. A causal mediation analysis with postintervention welfare tradeoff ratios as the mediator (10,000 nonparametric bootstrap samples, N = 1,061) supported partial mediation: the average causal mediation effect was significant, ACME = 0.73, 95% CI [0.26, 1.32], p = .002, corresponding to 7.1% of the total effect of SMR on donations (95% CI [2.5%, 13.2%]), with a large remaining direct effect, ADE = 9.65, 95% CI [6.88, 12.51], p < .001 (total effect = 10.38, 95% CI [7.57, 13.31]). Increased welfare weights thus account for a reliable but small share of SMR's effect on costly helping.

Are the Effects Mediated by an Increase in Impartial Beneficence?

A further candidate mechanism is that SMR made participants more impartially beneficent in general, and that transgender people benefited merely as one instance of a broadened moral concern. The data did not support this account at either step. First, SMR had no effect on endorsement of impartial beneficence relative to neutral reflection, b = -0.08, t(1038) = -1.01, p = .843 (one-tailed), dadj = -0.06, 95% CI [-0.18, 0.06], empathy induction, b = 0.06, p = .753 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.04, 95% CI [-0.08, 0.16], or perspective-taking, b = 0.01, p = .461 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.01, 95% CI [-0.11, 0.13]. Second, the preregistered mediation analysis found no evidence that impartial beneficence mediated the effect of SMR on donations to charities helping transgender people (10,000 bootstrap samples, N = 1,099): ACME = -0.33, 95% CI [-0.90, 0.19], p = .213, proportion mediated = -3.1%, while the direct effect remained large, ADE = 10.96, 95% CI [8.25, 13.75], p < .001.

Whatever SMR taught participants, it did not increase endorsement of impartial beneficence as an abstract principle.

Participants' Lessons Partially Mediated SMR's Effect on Outgroup Prosociality

Of the 582 participants retained in the SMR condition, 486 (83.5%) reported drawing a lesson from the reflection. These participants rated the lesson as clearly useful, with a mean well above the neutral point (M = 1.33 on a scale from -2 to +2, SD = 0.61), t(485) = 48.04, p < .001, d = 2.18, and reported strong intentions to apply it to future decisions (M = 1.35 on a scale from -2 to +2, SD = 0.73), t(485) = 40.76, p < .001, d = 1.85.

Our automatic thematic analysis of the lessons participants wrote down at the end of their systematic moral reflection found the following: 48.1% of participants in the SMR condition resolved to pay more attention to the consequences for distant others and prioritize people in need, vulnerable groups, and outcomes affecting many people; 43.3% resolved to think more analytically about future decisions; 38.9% resolved to take the perspective of those who will be affected by their decision and communicate and coordinate with the other people involved; 22.8% resolved to set boundaries to protect themselves from demands on their time and energy; 14.4% resolved to engage in practical, adaptive, and coordinated problem solving; 11.7% highlighted their moral values, moral principles, or moral rights; 10.3% resolved to prioritize their family; and the remaining 0.8% wrote responses that were too brief or too vague to be categorized. For a more detailed summary of the types of lessons participants learned in Experiment 3, see Table S2.1.4. During the reflection, participants also rated how much different strategies could have helped them reach a better decision on a scale from -2 to +2. Consistent with the content of participants' free text responses, the top-five strategies the average participant rated as most helpful were: i) following their moral compass (M = 1.62, 95% CI [1.51, 1.73]), ii) imagining how they would want to be treated if they were in the other people's position (M = 1.50, 95% CI [1.37, 1.62]), iii) ensuring fairness (M = 1.30, 95% CI [1.18, 1.43]), iv) choosing the action with the best consequences (M = 1.26, 95% CI [1.14, 1.38]), and v) pay more attention to the outcomes for strangers (M = 0.95, 95% CI [0.81, 1.08]).

A parallel-mediation analysis tested whether the effect of SMR on how much people were willing to donate to help transgender people after the intervention was mediated by the eight types of moral lessons while controlling for how much each participant had donated to help transgender people before the intervention (see Table S2.2.4 for full results). Together, the eight types of moral lessons mediated an estimated 55.8% of the total effect of SMR, p = .001, B = 5.556, SE = 1.397, 95% CI [2.781, 8.224], p < .001. The most important mediator was resolving to pay more attention to the consequences for distant others and to prioritize people in need, vulnerable groups, and outcomes affecting many people. It mediated about 24.7% of the effect of SMR (B = 2.464, SE = 0.892, 95% CI [0.796, 4.302], p = .006). In addition, the indirect effect through practical, adaptive, and coordinated problem solving was significant (B = 0.844, SE = 0.382, 95% CI [0.119, 1.626], p = .027). The remaining specific indirect effects were not significant (all ps ≥ .114). Reflection retained a significant positive direct effect after accounting for the mediators, B = 4.407, SE = 1.993, 95% CI [0.552, 8.423], p = .027, indicating partial rather than complete mediation. As in the other experiments, two independent human coders, who were blind to the AI-generated labels and to the mediation results, re-coded a sample of 150 lessons from the analytic sample, drawn to include all batch-eligible lessons the model had assigned to the practical problem-solving cluster, against the fixed cluster definitions.

Their codes reproduced the automatic assignments well for both clusters with significant indirect effects (practical problem-solving: mean Cohen's κ = .79; other-regarding assistance: mean κ = .60, with inter-coder agreement of κ = .83 and .72), supporting the reliability of the mediator measurements (see Sections S1.1.6 and S1.1.7 of the Supplemental Materials).

Effects on Effectiveness Focus

Effective versus ineffective help for the target group. Contrary to our prediction, SMR did not increase the proportion of a donation that participants allocated to an effective charity helping transgender people rather than an emotionally appealing but less effective way to help a transgender person. The allocation to the effective charity after SMR (56.4%) was not significantly different from the allocation after neutral reflection (57.7%), b = 0.36, t(842) = 0.17, p = .431 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.01, 95% CI [-0.12, 0.14], empathy induction, b = 3.16, t(1616) = 1.50, p = .067 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.10, 95% CI [-0.03, 0.23], or perspective-taking, b = 1.54, t(1616) = 0.73, p = .234 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.05, 95% CI [-0.08, 0.18].

Effective versus ineffective help for other groups. As predicted, SMR increased the effectiveness of participants' allocations of money between a highly cost-effective way to help immigrants/refugees and a less effective way. Participants who completed SMR allocated a larger share of a donation to an effective charity helping immigrants/refugees (63.6%) than participants in the neutral-reflection condition (61.1%), b = 3.35, t(839) = 1.78, p = .038 (onetailed), dadj = 0.12, 95% CI [-0.01, 0.25]. However, SMR did not increase the allocation to the effective charity relative to empathy induction, b = -1.06, t(1609) = -0.55, p = .709 (one-tailed), dadj = -0.04, 95% CI [-0.17, 0.09], or perspective-taking, b = -1.08, t(1609) = -0.56, p = .713 (one-tailed), dadj = -0.04, 95% CI [-0.17, 0.09], with both nonsignificant differences running opposite to the prediction.

Effective giving versus less effective support of transgender individuals. Likewise, when splitting a donation between an effective charity helping people in Africa and a less effective opportunity to help transgender people, participants in the SMR condition did not allocate a greater share to the effective charity than participants in the empathy-induction condition; the nonsignificant difference ran opposite to the prediction, b = -1.84, t(823) = -1.05, p = .853 (one-tailed), dadj = -0.06, 95% CI [-0.18, 0.06].

Moreover, according to an exploratory two-tailed analysis, the SMR group allocated a marginally smaller share of the donation to the effective global-health charity (M = 72.2%) than the neutral-reflection group (M = 75.4%, b = 3.05, t(1607) = 1.80, p = .073, dadj = 0.11, 95% CI [-0.01, 0.22]) or the perspective-taking group (M = 73.7%, b = 3.36, t(1607) = 1.96, p = .050, dadj = 0.12, 95% CI [-0.00, 0.24], see Table S2.12.3).

In sum, apart from a small increase in the effectiveness of giving for immigrants and refugees, SMR did not increase participants' focus on effectiveness in this experiment.

However, whether SMR teaches people to be more effectiveness-focused likely depends on the nature of the decision they are reflecting on, and this experiment used only a single scenario.

Results: Persistence After 7 or More Days

The follow-up assessments test the seventh prediction, that the effect of SMR on helping behavior persists for at least a week. Of the 2,310 participants retained in Part 1, 2,048 returned (88.7%), and retention did not differ across conditions, χ²(3) = 0.72, p = .869. Participants started Part 2 at least 7 days after completing Part 1. The average delay between the intervention and Part 2 was 8.4 days, and the median delay was 7.8 days. As summarized in Figure 5 and detailed below, we found that most effects of SMR on people's attitudes toward the target group, and some of its transfer effects, could still be detected 7 or more days later. Table S2.3.4, Panel B summarizes the descriptive statistics for all dependent variables at follow-up. Note. Each dot marks the covariate-adjusted standardized effect (Cohen's d) of SMR relative to neutral reflection (NR) on one outcome, immediately after the intervention (green) and at the follow-up 7 or more days later (purple); the gray segment connects the two estimates for the same outcome. Error bars are 90% confidence intervals, which correspond to the preregistered one-tailed tests with α = .05: an interval that excludes zero indicates a statistically significant one-tailed effect. Positive values denote change in the prosocial direction; the dehumanization measures (marked ↓) are reverse-coded, so positive values denote reduced dehumanization. The dashed vertical line marks no effect. WTR = welfare tradeoff ratio; Trans = transgender people; LGB = lesbian, gay, and bisexual people; dehum. = dehumanization. All estimates come from one mixed model for repeated measures per outcome, fitted to all available observations of the SMR and NR participants in the Part 1 sample; for the pro-trans donation, trans WTR, trans dehumanization, LGB WTR, and immigrant dehumanization, these are the preregistered models. Orange points show the effects about four and a half months later.

Persistent Improvement in Attitudes Towards the Target Group

As detailed below, 7 or more days later, the affect, cognitions, and behavior of participants who had engaged in SMR still demonstrated significantly more positive attitudes toward transgender people (see Figure 5).

Persistent Improvements in Behavior Toward the Target Group

Donations to charities helping the target group. The effect of SMR on donations to charities helping transgender people was attenuated but still present one week later. Adjusting for pre-reflection donations and gender, participants who had completed SMR donated significantly more money to charities helping transgender people than participants who had completed neutral reflection, b = 2.80, t(784) = 1.77, p = .038 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.08, 95% CI [-0.01, 0.18]. The retained effect (dadj = 0.08) was about one third of the immediate effect (dadj = 0.27), indicating that most of the effect decays over time.

Welfare tradeoff ratio. The effect of SMR on the weight participants placed on transgender people's welfare was fully sustained. One week after the intervention, participants in the SMR condition still exhibited higher welfare tradeoff ratios (M = 0.45) than participants in the neutral-reflection condition (M = 0.30), b = 0.12, t(903) = 3.81, p < .001 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.18, 95% CI [0.09, 0.27]. There was no indication that the effect had decayed. To the contrary, the estimated effect size had increased from dadj = 0.12 immediately following the intervention to dadj = 0.18 at follow-up.

Willingness to interact.

The initial effect of SMR on participants' willingness to form social relationships with transgender people (dadj = 0.11) persisted undiminished for 7 or more days, b = 0.17, t(947) = 2.52, p = .006 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.11, 95% CI [0.02, 0.19].

Anti-trans donation.

The unpredicted immediate increase in donations to charities seeking to restrict transgender people's rights did not persist. One week later, donations to these charities no longer differed between the SMR condition and the control group (NR), b = 1.12, t(784) = 0.55, p = .708 (one-tailed; p = .584, two-tailed), dadj = 0.03, 95% CI [-0.09, 0.15].

Persistent Improvement in Cognitions About the Target Group

Reduced dehumanization of transgender people. As predicted, the SMR-induced reduction in blatant dehumanization persisted. Adjusting for gender and baseline attitudes, participants in the SMR condition dehumanized transgender people significantly less than participants in the neutral-reflection condition, b = -5.83, t(947) = -3.85, p < .001 (one-tailed), dadj = -0.21, 95% CI [-0.31, -0.10]. There was no evidence of decay or attenuation: The follow-up effect was numerically larger than the immediate effect (dadj = -0.21 vs. -0.18).

Policy beliefs.

The SMR-induced increase in support for policies protecting transgender people was no longer significant at follow-up, b = 0.07, t(949) = 0.89, p = .187 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.04, 95% CI [-0.05, 0.13].

Moral-circle placement.

As immediately after the intervention, SMR had no effect on the placement of transgender people in participants' moral circles at follow-up, b = -0.01, t(923) = -0.76, p = .777 (one-tailed; p = .445, two-tailed), dadj = -0.04, 95% CI [-0.14, 0.06].

Perceived self-other overlap. One week later, the increase in perceived overlap with transgender people was numerically undiminished (M = 39.40 in the SMR condition vs. M = 36.25 in the control condition) but no longer statistically significant, b = 3.21, SE = 1.94, t(2002) = 1.65, p = .099, dadj = 0.11, 95% CI [-0.02, 0.23]. As in Part 1, neither the empathyinduction, b = 0.70, p = .719, nor the perspective-taking condition, b = 1.02, p = .602, increased perceived overlap relative to neutral reflection, and SMR did not differ significantly from either, b = 2.51, p = .191, and b = 2.19, p = .263. These tests were exploratory because perceived overlap was preregistered as a covariate rather than an outcome.

Effect on Warmth Toward the Target Group One Week Later

Warmth. One week after the intervention, the covariate-adjusted effect of SMR on warmth toward the target group was still significant according to the preregistered one-tailed test but attenuated, b = 1.93, t(996) = 1.87, p = .031 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.06, 95% CI [0.00, 0.12].

Numerically, the effect decreased to roughly half the size of the immediate effect (dadj = 0.13).

Mechanisms of Continued Attitude Change

The analyses presented in this section indicate that the SMR's persistent effect on donations was carried by the sustained increase in the weight participants placed on transgender people's welfare and the lessons participants had learned. By contrast, increases in perceived self-other overlap and in impartial beneficence did not account for it.

The persistent effects are not due to perceived self-other overlap alone. As in Part 1, we asked whether the effects that persisted at one week could be reduced to a sustained increase in perceived self-other overlap with transgender people. As preregistered (H1h), we re-estimated the follow-up models with perceived self-other overlap (measured at follow-up) as an additional covariate. The most robust persistent effects survived this adjustment: SMR still increased welfare tradeoff ratios, b = 0.11, t(902) = 3.52, p < .001 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.16, 95% CI [0.07, 0.25], and willingness to interact with transgender people, b = 0.11, t(946) = 1.74, p = .041 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.07, 95% CI [-0.01, 0.15].

However, the already attenuated donation effect was no longer significant after adjusting for perceived self-other overlap at follow-up, b = 2.08, t(783) = 1.36, p = .088 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.06, 95% CI [-0.03, 0.15], and the same was true of the follow-up effects on warmth, b = 1.06, t(995) = 1.07, p = .142 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.03, 95% CI [-0.03, 0.09], anti-trans donations, b = 1.68, t(783) = 0.83, p = .203 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.05, 95% CI [-0.07, 0.17].

Thus, the durable increases in the weight participants placed on transgender people's welfare and in their willingness to interact with them cannot be attributed to sustained perceived self-other overlap alone, whereas the smaller persistent effects could not be distinguished from an overlap-based pathway. Section S2.4 of the Supplemental Materials reports the corresponding analyses for the outcomes whose effects had not persisted to follow-up.

These findings have to be interpreted with caution for at least two reasons. First, we measured perceived self-other overlap at follow-up after the follow-up donation. Adjusting for a variable that the outcome itself influences can bias the estimated effect in either direction, because the covariate carries part of the intervention's effect on the outcome rather than being independent of it (Montgomery et al., 2018; Rosenbaum, 1984). Repeating the adjustment with the overlap measure from Part 1, which preceded the follow-up donations by one week, changed the estimate little but left it short of significance, b = 2.46, t(783) = 1.58, p = .058 (onetailed), compared with b = 2.80 for the unadjusted effect. The follow-up donation effect is small enough that whether it reaches significance depends on the covariates included: it is significant when the model adjusts simultaneously for all seven Part 1 measures of participants' feelings and beliefs about the outgroup, b = 3.92, t(672) = 2.51, p = .012, and not when perceived overlap alone is adjusted for (see Table S2.9.2). None of this fragility applies to the other persistent effects: the increases in welfare tradeoff ratios (p < .001) and in willingness to interact with transgender people (p = .021) survive adjustment for perceived overlap. Second, an attenuated coefficient is not, by itself, evidence that perceived self-other overlap is the pathway; we test that hypothesis directly below.

Was the persistent donation effect mediated by perceived self-other overlap?

We conducted an exploratory causal mediation analysis testing whether perceived self-other overlap with transgender people at follow-up mediated the effect of SMR on donations to protransgender charities one week later (10,000 nonparametric bootstrap samples, N = 752).

There was no reliable evidence of mediation: the indirect effect through perceived selfother overlap was not significant, ACME = 0.64, 95% CI [-0.26, 1.60], p = .159, and neither the direct effect, ADE = 1.91, 95% CI [-1.23, 5.02], p = .222, nor the total effect, 2.55, 95% CI [-0.72, 5.81], p = .123, reached significance in this subsample. The simultaneous multiple-mediator model reported in Table S2.9.3, which estimates this pathway alongside the welfare tradeoff ratio and six other measures of feelings and beliefs, likewise found no indirect effect through perceived self-other overlap (0.01, 95% CI [-0.21, 0.24], p = .935). Thus, at neither assessment did we find reliable evidence that perceived self-other overlap mediated the effect of SMR on costly helping; it appears to absorb variance in donations without carrying the intervention effect.

The sustained increase in WTRs mediated SMR's persistent effect on donations.

Because the increase in welfare tradeoff ratios was the most durable effect of SMR at follow-up, we conducted an exploratory causal mediation analysis testing whether the attenuated effect of SMR on donations to pro-transgender charities one week later was mediated by follow-up welfare tradeoff ratios, controlling for their pre-intervention level (10,000 nonparametric bootstrap samples, N = 712 participants with complete data on all measures). The indirect effect through welfare tradeoff ratios was significant, ACME = 0.61, 95% CI [0.09, 1.25], p = .019, whereas the remaining direct effect was not, ADE = 2.53, 95% CI [-0.71, 5.70], p = .128 (total effect = 3.15, 95% CI [-0.14, 6.43], p = .061). The point estimate for the proportion mediated was 19.5%, but it was imprecise (95% CI [-35.8%, 123.1%]), reflecting the small size of the total effect at follow-up. These results are consistent with the preregistered mediation observed immediately after the intervention and suggest that the durable increase in the weight participants placed on transgender people's welfare accounted for part of the remaining donation effect one week later.

Impartial beneficence did not mediate the effect of SMR. SMR did not increase endorsement of impartial beneficence at follow-up; the preregistered one-tailed test was clearly nonsignificant, b = -0.18, t(905) = -2.11, p = .983 (one-tailed). An exploratory two-tailed test indicated that participants in the SMR condition endorsed impartial beneficence less than participants in the neutral-reflection condition at follow-up, p = .035, dadj = -0.14, 95% CI [-0.26, -0.01]. However, a follow-up analysis indicated that this difference is not robust: a linear mixed model pooling the post-intervention and follow-up assessments showed that neither the average difference between conditions, b = -0.09, t(1174) = -1.09, p = .275, nor the Condition × Time interaction, b = -0.08, t(911) = -1.30, p = .192, was significant. Because we did not measure impartial beneficence at baseline, the apparent group difference at follow-up could reflect an imbalance between the two groups rather than an intervention effect. In conclusion, our data do not support the hypothesis that impartial beneficence mediated the effect of SMR on donations to pro-transgender charities.

Did the persistent donation effect depend on which lessons participants learned?

An exploratory parallel-mediation analysis controlling for pre-intervention donations tested whether SMR's effect on follow-up donations was mediated by how often seven themes appeared in participants' lessons. This analysis identified no significant specific indirect effects.

The largest was a positive indirect effect through perspective-sensitive, communicative, and collaborative judgment, B = 1.276, SE = 0.753, 95% CI [-0.208, 2.741], p = .090. The negative indirect effect through lessons demanding the prioritization of one's own family was also not significant, B = -0.557, SE = 0.346, 95% CI [-1.306, 0.077], p = .107. For further details on this mediation analysis, see Section S2.2.4.

SMR vs. Perspective-Taking and Empathy Induction 7 or More Days Later Behavior

Welfare Tradeoff Ratio. One week after the intervention, the clearest advantage of SMR over the established interventions was its effect on the weight participants placed on transgender people's welfare: SMR produced higher welfare tradeoff ratios for the target group than either empathy induction, b = 0.07, t(1794) = 2.26, p = .012 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.10, 95% CI [0.01, 0.19], or perspective-taking, b = 0.06, t(1794) = 1.91, p = .028 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.09, 95% CI [0.00, 0.18].

Donations.

For donations to charities helping transgender people, SMR's advantage over the established interventions faded away in the following 7 days relative to both empathy induction, p = .447, dadj = 0.01, 95% CI [-0.09, 0.10], and perspective-taking, p = .768, dadj = -0.03, 95% CI [-0.13, 0.06]. The difference between SMR and the comparison conditions also faded away for donations to charities seeking to restrict the rights of transgender people (all one-tailed ps ≥ .65, dadj = 0.06, 95% CI [-0.06, 0.18] for EI and dadj = 0.02, 95% CI [-0.10, 0.14] for PT).

Willingness to interact with transgender people. The same pattern held for willingness to interact with transgender people: At follow-up, there was no significant difference between the SMR condition and either empathy induction, p = .115, dadj = 0.05, 95% CI [-0.03, 0.13], or perspective-taking, p = .219, dadj = 0.03, 95% CI [-0.05, 0.11].

Cognitions

At follow-up, the SMR condition no longer significantly differed from either comparison condition with respect to policy support (one-tailed ps ≥ .18; dadj=0.02, 95% CI [-0.06, 0.11] for EI; dadj=0.00, 95% CI [-0.09, 0.09] for PT), moral-circle placement (one-tailed ps ≥ .34; dadj=0.00, 95% CI [-0.10, 0.09] for EI; dadj=0.02, 95% CI [-0.08, 0.12] for PT), or impartial beneficence (one-tailed ps ≥ 0.84, dadj = -0.07, 95% CI [-0.20, 0.05] for EI, and dadj = -0.07, 95% CI [-0.19, 0.06] for PT).

Feelings

At follow-up, SMR's immediate advantages had faded relative to empathy induction (p = .159, dadj = -0.03, 95% CI [-0.09, 0.03]) and perspective-taking (p = .170, dadj = -0.03, 95% CI [-0.09, 0.03]).

Further Follow-Up Analyses

Three further sets of follow-up analyses are reported in Section S2.13. First, most transfer effects had decayed by the follow-up, although the increase in the weight participants placed on the welfare of LGB people was fully sustained, dadj = 0.15, and the reduction in dehumanization of immigrants persisted; as immediately after the intervention, transfer did not extend to the moral-circle measures. Second, SMR had no effect on effectiveness-focused giving at follow-up, either relative to neutral reflection or relative to the two comparison interventions. Third, our preregistered prediction that the effects of SMR would decay more slowly than those of empathy induction was not supported for any of the eight outcomes tested; for donations to charities helping transgender people, an exploratory two-tailed test indicated a significantly steeper decline in the SMR condition.

Results: Persistence After Four and a Half Months

Two of the five preregistered primary predictions were supported (Section S2.14.2). After 4.5 months, the average participant in the SMR condition still donated $3.66 more to a charity helping transgender people than participants in the neutral-reflection condition, b = $3.66, t(668.5) = 1.83, p = .034 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.12, 95% CI [-0.01, 0.24]. This suggests that, after the difference between conditions had decayed from $9.80 to $3.40 within the first seven days, the effect on donations remained rather stable over the following four months. This effect does not appear to be specific to the target group. SMR participants also donated somewhat more money to a disaster-relief charity (b = $4.20, 95% CI [-$0.92, $9.33]) and to an arts charity (b = $2.33, 95% CI [-$2.35, $7.02]). The preregistered comparisons of the effects on these three donations were not significant (pro-transgender minus disaster relief: b = -$1.17, p = .683; disaster relief minus arts: b = $1.52, p = .219; both one-tailed), and neither was an exploratory comparison of the protransgender and arts donations (b = $0.49, 95% CI [-$3.80, $4.78], two-tailed p = .823).

Moreover, SMR participants did not give the pro-transgender charity a larger share when splitting a donation between it and an arts, disaster-relief, or local-community charity (bs from -$1.45 to -$0.31, all one-tailed ps ≥ .57). These findings suggest that the persistent effect of SMR on donations to the transgender charity may reflect a general increase in generosity rather than greater generosity toward transgender people in particular.

SMR's effect on the weight participants placed on transgender people's welfare did not decay (0.080 immediately after the intervention, 0.123 after 7 or more days, and 0.083 in Part 3; decay contrast b = 0.003, p = .960) and was of similar standardized size to the donation effect, but it was not significant in Part 3, b = 0.08, t(583.1) = 1.44, p = .075 (one-tailed), dadj = 0.10, 95% CI [-0.04, 0.23], partly because fewer participants returned to Part 3.

The reduction in the dehumanization of transgender people was smaller than in Parts 1 and 2 and no longer significant, b = -2.71, t(703.9) = -1.33, p = .092 (one-tailed), dadj = -0.09, 95% CI [-0.21, 0.04].

Other preregistered predictions.

Of the two transfer effects that had been present 7 or more days later, the reduction in the dehumanization of immigrants persisted, b = -2.82, t(697.8) = -1.99, p = .023 (one-tailed; Holm-corrected p = .047), dadj = -0.14, 95% CI [-0.28, -0.002], whereas the increase in the weight given to the welfare of LGB people was no longer significant (Holm-corrected p = .190). SMR had no detectable effects on warmth toward transgender people, willingness to interact with them, or signing the letter of support.

Unlike immediately after the intervention, SMR did not outperform empathy induction or perspective taking on any of the primary outcomes (see Supplement S2.14.4).

Moral concern.

Contrary to our prediction, SMR participants placed transgender people farther from the center of their circle of positive obligations, that is, the obligation to help them, than the neutral-reflection group, b = -0.21, t(616) = -2.60, p = .010 (two-tailed, exploratory), dadj = -0.21, 95% CI [-0.36, -0.05]. Exploratory analyses showed the same pattern for

LGB people and immigrants, but not for the other three types of moral concern or for and on the dehumanization of immigrants were still detectable, whereas the other effects were no longer significant. Fourth, because neutral reflection was matched for duration, difficulty, and deliberation, these benefits cannot be attributed to deliberation, elapsed time, or self-selection.

Fifth, SMR outperformed empathy induction and perspective-taking, which each deliver one of its ingredients, suggesting that reflecting on one's own past moral decision and drawing an explicit lesson contributed to the sustained improvement; isolating this component would require a factorial design with equally long comparison conditions.

Our mediation analyses suggested that SMR's effect on outgroup prosociality was more strongly mediated by an increase in how much weight people gave to the outgroup's welfare than by a revision of abstract moral principles, such as impartial beneficence, perceived selfother overlap, or the outgroup's placement in the participant's moral circle. This dissociation between enacted, target-directed concern and abstract measures of moral regard held across all three social groups and persisted over time. Part 3 sharpened this dissociation: four months after the intervention, SMR participants professed less obligation to help transgender people,

LGB people, and immigrants than neutral-reflection participants, even as they continued to donate slightly more to help transgender people. Consistent with a specific effect on the weight of the outgroup's welfare, SMR did not teach participants to prioritize more effective ways of helping over less effective ones in this experiment; given the related effects in Experiments 1, S1, and 2, this appears to reflect the content of the decision participants reflected on rather than SMR per se.

These conclusions are also qualified by our moral concern measure, an unvalidated adaptation of the moral expansiveness scale (Crimston et al., 2016). Participants placed each entity within a series of concentric circles centered on the self. Although the circles' written definitions matched Crimston et al.'s categories, participants may not have read them and may instead have placed the entities according to their psychological distance from the self. Its null results should therefore be interpreted with caution, and future work should use validated moral expansiveness measures, which brief compassion-based interventions have been shown to improve (Kirby et al., 2024).

Contrary to our predictions, SMR temporarily increased donations to charities seeking to protect cisgender women from transgender women. We return to this finding in the General Discussion, where it helps adjudicate between two accounts of SMR's mechanism.

Internal Meta-Analysis An internal meta-analysis pooling the standardized effect of SMR on the following decision across all five experiments (N = 4,186; see Section S2.8 of the Supplemental Materials) yielded a significant, small, positive effect, g = 0.27, 95% CI [0.12, 0.42]. However, the significant heterogeneity between the experiments, Q(4) = 24.48, p < .001, indicates that the true effect differed across experiments. That heterogeneity was entirely due to Experiment S2.

The effect sizes in the other four experiments, which all used the same ingroup in both decisions, were statistically indistinguishable from one another, Q(3) = 2.02, p = .568, I² = 0%.

For those experiments, the pooled effect size was small to moderate, g = 0.34, 95% CI [0.27, 0.41]. Adding a moderator for whether the ingroup was held constant accounted for virtually all of the between-experiment variance, contrast = 0.35, 95% CI [0.20, 0.49], R² = 100%. However, because this moderator was defined post-hoc and only one experiment lacked this property, this analysis describes the pattern we observed. This shows that the effect of systematic moral reflection is highly consistent across 4 of the 5, and that Experiment S2 falls outside of that range instead of sitting at its lower tail.

General Discussion

Table 5 summarizes how the seven predictions of our metacognitive moral learning account fared across the five preregistered experiments. S4). e Via the welfare tradeoff ratio; decision-process scales were not administered. f Mediation via the reduced importance of consequences for friends and family was supported; mediation via reliance on utilitarian reasoning was not. g Three of the preregistered process mediators were significant. h Mediation via welfare tradeoff ratios; the effect survived adjustment for seven measures of feelings and beliefs about the outgroup; mediation via impartial beneficence was not supported. i Transfer to donations, welfare tradeoff ratios, warmth, willingness to interact, and reduced dehumanization. Moral-circle placement was unaffected for the target group, so transfer could not be tested for this outcome. The preregistered reduction in donations to charities opposing transgender rights was not supported (see the Results). j Supported for donations immediately after the intervention; at follow-up, SMR's advantage persisted for the weight given to transgender people's welfare but not for donations. k Both behavioral effects persisted: the increase in welfare tradeoff ratios was undiminished, the donation effect was attenuated but remained significant. At the fourmonth follow-up, a small donation effect remained significant (one-tailed), whereas the welfare-tradeoff-ratio effect, although undiminished, was no longer significant (Section S2.14). The other effects, which this prediction does not cover, varied.

Consistent with Prediction 1, systematic moral reflection increased outgroup prosociality in four of the five experiments, whether measured through hypothetical volunteering or donation decisions or real, incentive-compatible donations to charities helping a stigmatized group (Experiment 3). Consistent with Prediction 2, worse evaluations of the first decision predicted larger changes in outgroup prosociality across all five experiments. Consistent with Prediction 3, systematic moral reflection taught participants to more deliberately consider how their choices affect distant others. This change in participants' decision strategies is the signature of metacognitive moral learning. Supporting Prediction 4, these changes in participants' decision processes consistently mediated SMR's effect on outgroup prosociality.

Consistent with Prediction 5, SMR's effects transferred to outgroups absent from the original dilemma and to other types of decisions (Experiments 2 and 3). The only exception was Experiment S2. Its second decision recast the first decision's outgroup-food-insecure Americans, whom some participants had resolved to prioritize-as more similar to the second decision's ingroup than to its outgroup. This suggests that what reflection cultivates is concern for a category of people, and that concern does not carry over to beneficiaries the learner categorizes differently. Because social categorization varies across individuals, SMR may have taught some participants to care more about fellow Americans in need and others to care more about distant strangers, consistent with our finding that SMR increased the variance of the second decision without shifting its mean. Transfer thus appears to depend on how the groups in a new situation map onto those in the decision the person reflected on, rather than on social distance alone.

Consistent with Prediction 6, SMR increased donations to charities helping the outgroup significantly more than the perspective-taking or empathy-induction interventions in Part 1. In Part 2, those differences were no longer significant, but the SMR group continued to give significantly more weight to the outgroup's welfare than the comparison groups. Moreover, confirming Prediction 7, the effect of SMR on behavioral measures of outgroup prosociality, including donations and welfare tradeoff ratios, remained statistically significant seven or more days after the intervention. A preregistered second follow-up found that a small effect on donations, and the transfer effect on the dehumanization of immigrants, persisted for about four and a half months.

Theoretical Implications

Prior work explained the benefits of empathy induction and perspective-taking in terms of attitudes, such as greater moral concern for the outgroup, or a sense of oneness with one of its members. Our findings show that attitude change is not the only pathway. Learning-induced changes in the strategies people use to make social decisions constitute another pathway with a distinct mechanism: people learn to decide in ways that align their choices with the moral concern they already have. SMR consistently increased the attention participants paid to potential consequences for distant others and the weight they put on those consequences. Its effects on abstract moral beliefs were weaker and inconsistent. Exploratory follow-up analyses reinforced this conclusion. In Experiment 3, SMR increased costly helping more than an empathy induction did while producing less empathic concern than either the empathy induction or the neutral-reflection control. Its effect on donations one week later survived adjustment for all post-intervention measures of participants' feelings and beliefs about the outgroup; the weight participants assigned to the outgroup's welfare was the only pathway that carried a significant indirect effect. A very similar pattern appeared in Experiment 2, the only other experiment that measured participants' beliefs about the outgroup. The moral learning SMR produces therefore appears to operate primarily on the learner's decision-making system rather than on their beliefs about morality. This may explain why several effective prejudice-reduction interventions improve behavior toward an outgroup without changing beliefs or feelings about it (Paluck et al., 2021). Applying computational models of metacognitive moral learning (Maier et al., 2025; Spiteri, Maier, et al., 2026) to intergroup contact (e.g., Woodley et al., 2025) might therefore yield a mechanistic theory of the conditions contact must meet to increase outgroup prosociality and reduce intergroup hostility.

The temporary rise in anti-transgender giving is also informative about the mechanism.

Improved attitudes toward transgender people should, if anything, reduce support for organizations seeking to restrict transgender rights, such as the Women's Liberation Front (WoLF). By contrast, an increase in the weight given to the needs of whoever a decision presents as vulnerable predicts the opposite, because every charity we used presented itself as protecting a vulnerable group: organizations like WoLF ask potential donors to help them protect women and girls from transgender people. The observed pattern fits the second account, and prioritizing those in greatest need was among the most common themes in participants' lessons across Experiments 1-3.

Practical Implications

Because a single round of SMR had small to moderate effects, repeated exercises spanning different types of intergroup conflict may be needed for the benefits to reach real-world behavior. Because transfer depends on how closely the two decisions' groups correspond, practitioners should apply SMR to decisions resembling those they wish to improve. Applied this way, SMR could support ethics training in educational and organizational settings as well as personal ones. More research is needed to identify and rectify potential failure modes, such as the observed increase in donations to organizations seeking to restrict the target group's rights.

Alternative Explanations

A skeptical reader might object that Experiments 1 and S1 show only that rewarding a specific behavior reinforces it, or that SMR merely improves attitudes toward one specific stakeholder.

Neither fits the wider pattern. Participants consistently learned to rely more on general moral principles that require them to consider their choices' potential effects on distant others.

Moreover, reflecting on a volunteering decision transferred to subsequent donation decisions (Experiments 2 and 3) and helping groups that were absent from the original situation.

A skeptical reader might also worry that our findings merely reflect social desirability.

Several pieces of evidence speak against this interpretation. First, the effects transferred to incentive-compatible donations, where participants gave up their own money for the stakeholders whose welfare they had reflected on, echoing earlier findings that moral learning transferred to an incentive-compatible donation in a separate experiment run by a different, seemingly unrelated experimenter (Maier et al., 2026). Second, the empathy-induction and perspective-taking conditions of Experiment 3 matched SMR plausibly signaled the experimenter's prosocial values at least as strongly, yet SMR produced larger effects on costly helping and warmth, which a demand-based account would not predict. Third, very few participants articulated the predicted direction of our effects (at most 5.9% in any condition).

Significant condition differences occurred only in Experiment 1 and in the four-month follow-up of Experiment 3, and excluding those participants left the effects virtually unchanged (d = 0.40 vs. d = 0.43 in Experiment 1; dadj = 0.12 vs. 0.10 for donations in the follow-up, although the latter then narrowly missed significance, one-tailed p = .053). This evidence also fits a broader methodological literature showing that demand effects in online experiments are substantially smaller than in in-person studies, and well below the pooled effect of SMR on the decision that followed it (Coles et al., 2025; Woodley et al., 2026; see Section S2.5 of the Supplemental Materials).

Our finding that the increase in outgroup prosociality correlated with feeling bad about a previous, less outgroup-prosocial decision suggests another alternative explanation: participants might have helped more to repair their mood (Cialdini & Kenrick, 1976). However, an affective state induced in a single online session is unlikely to still be operating a week later, so this cannot explain why SMR still increased outgroup prosociality seven or more days later, let alone four months later. Moreover, Experiment 3's empathy-induction condition elicited significantly more empathic concern than SMR, and plausibly at least as much negative affect, yet produced smaller donations.

A further alternative explanation is that SMR did not increase outgroup prosociality specifically but simply made people more willing to give money away; indeed, it increased total giving across the pro-and anti-transgender charities (dadj = 0.32). However, this effect was not reducible to generalized giving because the balance of donations shifted toward the charities supporting transgender people (dadj = 0.12), and only the pro-transgender increase persisted one week later.

Limitations and Future Directions

The Statement of Limitations preceding this article lists the limitations of this research.

Most consequential for the account developed here, the mediating roles of the lessons participants drew and of the weight they gave the outgroup's welfare were inferred from mediation analyses rather than manipulated; future experiments could corroborate the proposed mechanism by teaching those lessons directly. A further open question is whether the benefits of SMR survive outside the laboratory. All five experiments were conducted online, and effects in this literature are consistently smaller in the field than in the laboratory (Paluck et al., 2021); so whether SMR retains its benefits when delivered in a classroom, workplace, or community setting is the most important open question about its practical value. That said, only 7% of prejudice-reduction studies measure actual behavior and only 8% measure any outcome beyond the immediate post-test (Paluck et al., 2021). By contrast, Experiment 3 measured incentive-compatible donations and re-measured them one week later. Moreover, our metaanalytic estimate across five large preregistered samples, g = 0.27, 95% CI [0.12, 0.42], is consistent with the range for the best-powered prejudice-reduction studies, suggesting that it is not likely inflated by the small-study effects that characterize much of this literature (Paluck et al., 2021).

Broader Significance

Our findings point to a route to outgroup prosociality that differs from the attitude-change and prejudice-reduction approaches dominating intergroup research: rather than altering what people believe and feel about a group, SMR teaches them to decide differently: to think about and give more weight to the needs of distant others. Because people increasingly turn to technology to reflect on social interactions (Hao et al., 2025), prompting large language models to facilitate systematic moral reflection could deliver SMR interventions at scale. SMR could likewise enrich social-emotional learning activities in schools (Weissberg et al., 2015). In addition to further research, realizing this potential will likely require repeated practice, pedagogically chosen scenarios, and careful scaffolding.

Results: Immediate Effects

We first report the effects measured immediately after the intervention and then examine whether those effects persisted 7 or more days later and about four and a half months later. As summarized in Figure 3, we found that SMR improved people's attitudes toward both the target group (transgender people) and other social groups (immigrants and LGB people). The descriptive statistics of all dependent variables at post-test are summarized in Table S2.3.4, Panel A.

SMR Improved Attitudes Towards the Target Group

We assessed the effects of SMR on people's attitudes toward the target group within the affect-behavior-cognition framework of attitudes research. We found that all three components

The Effects of Empathy Induction and SMR did not transfer to other groups Neither comparison intervention transferred to other groups. Relative to neutral reflection, neither empathy induction nor perspective taking significantly improved any of the eight measures of attitudes and behavior toward immigrants and LGB people. The only significant effect was a decrease in moral concern for LGB people following perspective taking, dadj = -0.12, p = .024. All other effects were non-significant, although empathy induction had marginal effects on the dehumanization of immigrants (dadj = -0.11, p = .051) and warmth toward immigrants (dadj = 0.05, p = .056). For a complete summary of these exploratory two-tailed tests, see Table S2.12.1.

In direct, two-tailed comparisons with perspective taking, SMR produced significantly greater transfer effects on willingness to interact with immigrants, dehumanization of immigrants, warmth toward immigrants, and the weight placed on the welfare of LGB people (dadj = 0.10 to 0.15, all ps < .01). In direct two-tailed comparisons with empathy induction, SMR achieved significantly greater transfer effects on the welfare tradeoff ratios for immigrants (dadj = 0.11, p = .004) and for LGB people (dadj = 0.08, p =.023). Neither comparison intervention outperformed SMR on any measure, and every point estimate favored SMR except those for the two moral-circle measures, on which no condition differed from any other (see Table S2.12.2).

These analyses were not preregistered; their consistency across measures is more informative than any individual test.

Summary of Transfer Effects

In summary, the benefits of SMR transferred beyond the target group to two outgroups the intervention never mentioned, and they did so with the same signature observed for transgender people: reliable improvements in concrete, enacted forms of concern (i.e., donations, welfare tradeoffs, willingness to interact, warmth, and (reduced) dehumanization), but without a significant effect on the outgroup's moral circle placement. Neither of the two members of the immediate family (Section S2.14.5). Placing transgender people closer to the center of one's circle of positive obligations was associated with larger donations to the charity helping them to a similar degree in both conditions (r = .44 after SMR and .43 after neutral reflection). Thus, four months after the intervention, SMR participants professed less obligation to help these groups while donating somewhat more to help one of them.

Because the measure was adapted for this study and lacks a pre-intervention baseline, this finding should be taken with a grain of salt.

Robustness and hypothesis awareness. The estimated effects were similar across the preregistered robustness analyses (Section S2.14.4). In the funnel debriefing, 2.2% of participants articulated the predicted direction of the effects, more often after SMR (4.3%) than after neutral reflection (1.3%), p = .029 (Fisher's exact test). A validation of this coding by two independent human coders, who are blind to condition and to the automatic codes, is under way (Section S2.14.7). Excluding the 18 hypothesis-aware SMR and neutral-reflection participants changed the donation effect only slightly, b = $3.26, dadj = 0.10, but its one-tailed p value rose to .053, just above the threshold of significance. Taken together, Part 3 suggests that a small part of SMR's effect on donations persisted for months, as did its transfer effect on the dehumanization of immigrants, but both estimates lie close to the threshold of statistical significance. For further information on the results from Part 3 see Section S2.14.3 in the Supplementary Material.

Discussion

Experiment 3 extended the findings of Experiments 1-2 in five preregistered ways. First, SMR increased real, incentive-compatible donations to charities helping a stigmatized group and improved affective, cognitive, and behavioral attitude measures. Second, these benefits transferred to immigrants and LGB people. Third, the gains in welfare tradeoff ratios, willingness to interact, and reduced dehumanization were undiminished 7 or more days later, and the donation effect remained significant. Four and a half months later, small effects on donations

Part 2.

Starting seven days after their Part 1 session, we invited all participants who completed Part 1 seven days earlier to Part 2 on a rolling basis, regardless of whether they met the Part 1 data-quality criteria. In total, 2,208 participants completed Part 2. Of these, 55 were excluded based on the same preregistered data-quality criteria of Part 2 (failing more than 1 of the comprehension and attention checks or speeding), and a further 146 were excluded for not meeting the inclusion criteria of Part 1 (including participants affected by the technical error).

Part 3.

About four and a half months after Part 1, we invited all participants from the Part 1 analytic sample whose Prolific accounts were still active (2,308 of 2,310), regardless of whether they had completed Part 2. Of these 2,308 people, 1,299 participated in Part 3 (56.3%), and the return rate did not differ across conditions (55.5%-57.1%), χ 2 (3) = 0.40, p = .94. The median interval between a participant's Part 1 session and their Part 3 submission was slightly larger than 4.5 months (Mdn = 138 days, interquartile range: 134-140 days). After the preregistered exclusions for duplicate submissions, failed attention checks, and failed authenticity checks (see Section S1.7.2 of the Supplemental Materials), the Part 3 sample comprised 1,248 participants (314 SMR, 306 neutral reflection, 326 empathy induction, 302 perspective taking). ProcedureFor Part 1, we posted two mutually exclusive Prolific HITs with identical names in parallel (1200 participants each). Participants who accepted the first HIT were randomly assigned to either the SMR condition or the neutral reflection control condition. Participants who accepted the second HIT were randomly assigned to one of the two comparison conditions designed to improve people's attitudes toward transgender people with established social psychological interventions. The interventions used in these comparison conditions were an empathy induction based onBatson, Polycarpou, et al. (1997) and a perspective-taking intervention based onDovidio et al. (2004).

Foundation) and an

organization preserving or celebrating transgender history and culture (the NYC Trans Oral History Project, Trans Pride Washington DC, or the Museum of Transology); ii) an effective charity providing material, medical, or legal support to thousands of transgender people (Point of Pride, GLBTQ Legal Advocates & Defenders, or the Transgender Law Center) and a transgender archive or festival (the Louise Lawrence Transgender Archive, the San Francisco Transgender Film Festival, or the Transgender Archives at the University of Victoria); and iii) an effective charity serving thousands of refugees or immigrants (the International Refugee Assistance Program, Refugees International, or RAICES) and an organization commemorating the history of migration (the Undocumented Migration Project, the Tenement Museum, or the Angel Island Immigration Station Foundation). Before each decision, participants read a factual description of both organizations; the descriptions of the less effective options stated explicitly that the organization does not provide direct services, financial support, or healthcare to any individual. For each decision, we measured effectiveness focus by the proportion of the $100 allocated to the highly effective charity.
